**France**,
`{{Efn|{{IPA|fr|fʁɑ̃s|lang|LL-Q150 (fra)-Fhala.K-France.wav}}<!-- Do not add English pronunciation per [[Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Lead Section]]. -->}}`
{=mediawiki}
officially the **French
Republic**,
`{{Efn|{{Langx|fr|link=no|République française}} {{IPA|fr|ʁepyblik fʁɑ̃sɛːz|lang|République Française (versión alternativa).wav}}}}`
{=mediawiki}
is a country primarily located in [Western
Europe](Western_Europe "wikilink").
`{{efn|According to the boundaries of Western Europe used by among others the [[United Nations geoscheme]], [[CIA World Factbook]], and [[Eurovoc]], and going by the Cold War [[NATO]]-[[Warsaw Pact]] divide}}`
{=mediawiki}
[Its overseas regions and territories](Overseas_France "wikilink")
include [French Guiana](French_Guiana "wikilink") in [South
America](South_America "wikilink"), [Saint Pierre and
Miquelon](Saint_Pierre_and_Miquelon "wikilink") in the [North
Atlantic](North_Atlantic "wikilink"), the [French West
Indies](French_West_Indies "wikilink"), and [many
islands](List_of_islands_of_France "wikilink") in
[Oceania](Oceania "wikilink") and the [Indian
Ocean](Indian_Ocean "wikilink"). [Metropolitan
France](Metropolitan_France "wikilink") shares borders with
[Belgium](Belgium "wikilink") and [Luxembourg](Luxembourg "wikilink") to
the north; [Germany](Germany "wikilink") to the northeast;
[Switzerland](Switzerland "wikilink") to the east;
[Italy](Italy "wikilink") and [Monaco](Monaco "wikilink") to the
southeast; [Andorra](Andorra "wikilink") and [Spain](Spain "wikilink")
to the south; and a maritime border with the [United
Kingdom](United_Kingdom "wikilink") to the northwest. Its metropolitan
area extends from the [Rhine](Rhine "wikilink") to the [Atlantic
Ocean](Atlantic_Ocean "wikilink") and from the [Mediterranean
Sea](Mediterranean_Sea "wikilink") to the [English
Channel](English_Channel "wikilink") and the [North
Sea](North_Sea "wikilink"). Its 18 [integral
regions](Regions_of_France#List_of_administrative_regions "wikilink")---five
of which are overseas---span a combined area of
`{{Cvt|632702|km2}}`{=mediawiki}, with [a total
population](Demographics_of_France "wikilink") estimated at over 69.1
million in 2026. Its capital, [largest
city](List_of_communes_in_France_with_over_20,000_inhabitants "wikilink")
and main cultural and economic centre is [Paris](Paris "wikilink").

Metropolitan France was settled during the [Iron
Age](Iron_Age "wikilink") by [Celtic
tribes](List_of_ancient_Celtic_peoples_and_tribes "wikilink") known as
[Gauls](Gauls "wikilink") before [Rome annexed the
area](Roman_Gaul "wikilink") in 51 BC, leading to a distinct
[Gallo-Roman culture](Gallo-Roman_culture "wikilink"). In the [Early
Middle Ages](Early_Middle_Ages "wikilink"), the
[Franks](Franks "wikilink") formed the kingdom of
[Francia](Francia "wikilink"), which became the heartland of the
[Carolingian Empire](Carolingian_Empire "wikilink"). The [Treaty of
Verdun](Treaty_of_Verdun "wikilink") of 843 partitioned the empire, with
[West Francia](West_Francia "wikilink") evolving into the [Kingdom of
France](Kingdom_of_France "wikilink"). In the [High Middle
Ages](High_Middle_Ages "wikilink"), France was a powerful but
decentralised [feudal](Feudalism "wikilink") kingdom, but from the
mid-14th to the mid-15th centuries, France was plunged into a dynastic
conflict with [England](Kingdom_of_England "wikilink") known as the
[Hundred Years\' War](Hundred_Years'_War "wikilink"). In the 16th
century, [French culture](Culture_of_France "wikilink") flourished
during the [French Renaissance](French_Renaissance "wikilink"), and a
[French colonial empire](French_colonial_empire "wikilink") emerged.
Internally, France was dominated by the conflict with the [House of
Habsburg](House_of_Habsburg "wikilink") and the [French Wars of
Religion](French_Wars_of_Religion "wikilink") between
[Catholics](Catholics "wikilink") and [Huguenots](Huguenots "wikilink").
France was successful in the [Thirty Years\'
War](Thirty_Years'_War "wikilink") and further increased its influence
during the reign of [Louis XIV](Louis_XIV "wikilink").

The [French Revolution](French_Revolution "wikilink") of 1789 overthrew
the `{{Lang|fr|[[Ancien Régime]]|italic=no}}`{=mediawiki} and produced
the [Declaration of the Rights of
Man](Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen "wikilink"),
which expresses the nation\'s ideals to this day. France reached its
political and military zenith in the early 19th century under [Napoleon
Bonaparte](Napoleon "wikilink"), subjugating much of continental Europe
and establishing the [First French
Empire](First_French_Empire "wikilink"). Its collapse initiated a period
of relative decline during which France endured the [Bourbon
Restoration](Bourbon_Restoration_in_France "wikilink") until the
founding of the [French Second
Republic](French_Second_Republic "wikilink"), which was succeeded by the
[Second French Empire](Second_French_Empire "wikilink") upon [Napoleon
III](Napoleon_III "wikilink")\'s takeover. His empire collapsed during
the [Franco-Prussian War](Franco-Prussian_War "wikilink") in 1870. This
led to the establishment of the [French Third
Republic](French_Third_Republic "wikilink"), and a period of economic
prosperity and cultural and scientific flourishing known as the [Belle
Époque](Belle_Époque "wikilink"). France was one of the [major
participants](Triple_Entente "wikilink") of [World War
I](World_War_I "wikilink"), from which [it emerged
victorious](Treaty_of_Versailles "wikilink") at great human and economic
cost. It was among the [Allies of World War
II](Allies_of_World_War_II "wikilink"), but it surrendered and [was
occupied](German_military_administration_in_occupied_France_during_World_War_II "wikilink")
by [Germany](Nazi_Germany "wikilink") in 1940. Following [its liberation
in 1944](Liberation_of_France "wikilink"), the short-lived [Fourth
Republic](French_Fourth_Republic "wikilink") was established and later
dissolved over the course of the [Algerian
War](Algerian_War "wikilink"). The current [Fifth
Republic](French_Fifth_Republic "wikilink"), a
[semi-presidential](Semi-presidential_system "wikilink") system, was
formed in 1958 by [Charles de Gaulle](Charles_de_Gaulle "wikilink").
[Algeria](Algeria "wikilink") and most French colonies became
independent in the 1960s, with the majority retaining [close economic
and military ties with France](Françafrique "wikilink").

France retains its centuries-long status as a global centre [of
art](French_art "wikilink"),
[science](Science_and_technology_in_France "wikilink"),
[cuisine](French_cuisine "wikilink") and
[philosophy](French_philosophy "wikilink"). It hosts the [fourth-largest
number](World_Heritage_Sites_by_country "wikilink") of UNESCO [World
Heritage Sites](World_Heritage_Sites "wikilink"), with [54 in
total](List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_France "wikilink"), and is the
[world\'s leading tourist
destination](World_Tourism_rankings "wikilink"), having received
102 million foreign [visitors in 2025](Tourism_in_France "wikilink"). A
[developed country](developed_country "wikilink"), France has a [high
nominal per capita income
globally](<List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal)_per_capita> "wikilink"), and
[its economy](Economy_of_France "wikilink") ranks among the largest in
the world [by both nominal
GDP](<List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal)> "wikilink") and [PPP-adjusted
GDP](<List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)> "wikilink"). It is a [great
power](great_power "wikilink"), being one of the five [permanent members
of the United Nations Security
Council](permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council "wikilink")
and an official [nuclear-weapon
state](List_of_states_with_nuclear_weapons "wikilink"). The country is
part of [multiple international organisations and
forums](Outline_of_France#International_organisation_membership "wikilink").

## Etymology

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Name of France}}
```

Originally applied to the whole [Frankish Empire](Francia "wikilink"),
the name _France_ comes from the [Latin](Latin "wikilink")
`{{Lang|la|[[Francia]]}}`{=mediawiki}, or \'realm of the
[Franks](Franks "wikilink")\'.[^9] The [name of the
Franks](name_of_the_Franks "wikilink") is related to the English word
_frank_ (\'free\'): the latter stems from the [Old
French](Old_French "wikilink") `{{Lang|ang|franc}}`{=mediawiki} (\'free,
noble, sincere\'), and ultimately from the [Medieval
Latin](Medieval_Latin "wikilink") word _francus_ (\'free, exempt from
service; a freeman, a Frank\'), a generalisation of the tribal name that
emerged as a [Late Latin](Late_Latin "wikilink") borrowing of the
reconstructed [Frankish](Frankish_language "wikilink")
[endonym](Endonym_and_exonym "wikilink")
`{{Lang|frk|*Frank}}`{=mediawiki}.[^10][^11] It has been suggested that
the meaning \'free\' was adopted because after the conquest of
[Gaul](Gaul "wikilink"), only Franks were free of taxation,[^12] or more
generally because they had the status of freemen in contrast to servants
or slaves.[^13] The etymology of _\*Frank_ is uncertain. It is
traditionally derived from the
[Proto-Germanic](Proto-Germanic_language "wikilink") word
`{{Lang|gem-x-proto|frankōn}}`{=mediawiki}, which translates as
\'javelin\' or \'lance\' (the throwing axe of the Franks was known as
the _[francisca](francisca "wikilink")_),[^14] although these weapons
may have been named because of their use by the Franks, not the other
way around.[^15]

In English, \'France\' is pronounced `{{IPAc-en|f|r|æ|n|s}}`{=mediawiki}
`{{Respell|FRANSS}}`{=mediawiki} in American English and
`{{IPAc-en|f|r|ɑː|n|s}}`{=mediawiki} `{{Respell|FRAHNSS}}`{=mediawiki}
or `{{IPAc-en|f|r|æ|n|s}}`{=mediawiki} `{{Respell|FRANSS}}`{=mediawiki}
in British English. The pronunciation with `{{IPAc-en|ɑː}}`{=mediawiki}
is mostly confined to accents with the [trap--bath
split](trap–bath_split "wikilink") such as [Received
Pronunciation](Received_Pronunciation "wikilink"), though it can be also
heard in some other dialects such as [Cardiff
English](Cardiff_English "wikilink").[^16]

## History

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|History of France}}
```

### Prehistory

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Prehistory of France}}
```

The oldest traces of archaic humans in what is now France date from
approximately 1.8 million years ago.[^17]
[Neanderthals](Neanderthal "wikilink") occupied the region into the
[Upper Paleolithic](Upper_Paleolithic "wikilink") era but were slowly
replaced by _[Homo sapiens](Human "wikilink")_ around 35,000 BC.[^18]
This period witnessed the emergence of [cave
painting](cave_painting "wikilink") in the
[Dordogne](Dordogne "wikilink") and [Pyrenees](Pyrenees "wikilink"),
including at [Lascaux](Lascaux "wikilink"), dated to
`{{Circa|18,000}}`{=mediawiki} BC.[^19] At the end of the [Last Glacial
Period](Last_Glacial_Period "wikilink") (10,000 BC), the climate became
milder;[^20] from approximately 7,000 BC, this part of Western Europe
entered the [Neolithic](Neolithic "wikilink") era, and its inhabitants
became [sedentary](Sedentism "wikilink").

After demographic and agricultural development between the 4th and 3rd
millennia BC, [metallurgy appeared](Three-age_system "wikilink"),
initially working gold, [copper](Chalcolithic "wikilink") and
[bronze](Bronze_Age "wikilink"), then later
[iron](Iron_Age "wikilink").[^21] France has numerous
[megalithic](megalith "wikilink") sites from the Neolithic, including
the [Carnac stones](Carnac_stones "wikilink") site (approximately 3,300
BC).

### Antiquity (6th century BC -- 5th century AD) {#antiquity_6th_century_bc_5th_century_ad}

In 600 BC, [Ionian](Ionia "wikilink")
[Greeks](Greeks_in_pre-Roman_Gaul "wikilink") from
[Phocaea](Phocaea "wikilink") founded the colony of
[Massalia](Massalia "wikilink") (present-day
[Marseille](Marseille "wikilink")).[^22] [Celtic
tribes](Celtic_tribes "wikilink") penetrated parts of eastern and
northern France, spreading through the rest of the country between the
5th and 3rd century BC.[^23] Around 390 BC, the Gallic chieftain
[Brennus](<Brennus_(leader_of_the_Senones)> "wikilink") and his troops
made their way to [Roman Italy](Roman_Italy "wikilink"), defeated the
Romans in the [Battle of the Allia](Battle_of_the_Allia "wikilink"), and
besieged and ransomed Rome.[^24] This left Rome weakened, and the Gauls
continued to harass the region until 345 BC when they entered into a
peace treaty.[^25] But the Romans and the Gauls remained adversaries for
centuries.[^26] ![The [Maison carrée](Maison_carrée "wikilink") was a
temple of the [Gallo-Roman](Gallo-Roman_culture "wikilink") city of
[Nemausus](Nemausus "wikilink") (present-day [Nîmes](Nîmes "wikilink"))
and is one of the best-preserved [Roman
temples](Roman_temple "wikilink")
anywhere.](<France-002364_-_Square_House_(15867600545).jpg> "The Maison carrée was a temple of the Gallo-Roman city of Nemausus (present-day Nîmes) and is one of the best-preserved Roman temples anywhere.")
Around 125 BC, the south of Gaul was conquered by the Romans, who called
this region
`{{Lang|la|[[Gallia Narbonensis|Provincia Nostra]]}}`{=mediawiki} (\"Our
Province\"), which evolved into [Provence](Provence "wikilink") in
French.[^27] [Julius Caesar](Julius_Caesar "wikilink") conquered the
remainder of Gaul and overcame a revolt by Gallic chieftain
[Vercingetorix](Vercingetorix "wikilink") in 52 BC.[^28] Gaul was
divided by [Augustus](Augustus "wikilink") into provinces,[^29] and many
cities were founded during the [Gallo-Roman
period](Roman_Gaul "wikilink"), including
[Lugdunum](Lugdunum "wikilink") (present-day [Lyon](Lyon "wikilink")),
the capital of the Gauls.[^30] In 250--290 AD, Roman Gaul suffered a
crisis with its [fortified borders](<Limes_(Roman_Empire)> "wikilink")
attacked by barbarians.[^31] The situation improved in the first half of
the 4th century, a period of revival and prosperity.[^32] In 312,
Emperor [Constantine I](Constantine_the_Great "wikilink") [converted to
Christianity](Constantine_the_Great_and_Christianity "wikilink").
Christians, who had been persecuted, increased.[^33] But from the 5th
century, the [barbarian invasions](Migration_Period "wikilink")
resumed.[^34] [Teutonic](Teutons "wikilink") tribes invaded the region,
the [Visigoths](Visigoths "wikilink") settling in the southwest, the
[Burgundians](Burgundians "wikilink") along the [Rhine
Valley](Rhine_Valley "wikilink"), and the Franks in the north.[^35]

### Early Middle Ages (5th--10th century) {#early_middle_ages_5th10th_century}

In [late antiquity](late_antiquity "wikilink"), Gaul was divided into
Germanic kingdoms and a remaining Gallo-Roman territory. [Celtic
Britons](Celtic_Britons "wikilink"), fleeing the [Anglo-Saxon settlement
of Britain](Anglo-Saxon_settlement_of_Britain "wikilink"), settled in
west [Armorica](Armorica "wikilink"); the Armorican peninsula was
renamed [Brittany](Brittany "wikilink"), and [Celtic
culture](Celts "wikilink") was revived.

The first leader to unite all Franks was [Clovis
I](Clovis_I "wikilink"), who began his reign as king of the [Salian
Franks](Salian_Franks "wikilink") in 481, routing the last forces of the
Roman governors in 486. Clovis said he would be baptised a Christian in
the event of victory against the [Visigothic
Kingdom](Visigothic_Kingdom "wikilink"), which was said to have
guaranteed the battle. Clovis [regained the southwest from the
Visigoths](Franco-Visigothic_Wars "wikilink") and was baptised in 508.
Clovis was the first [Germanic](Germanic_peoples "wikilink") conqueror
after the [Fall of the Western Roman
Empire](Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire "wikilink") to convert to
Catholic Christianity; thus France was given the title \"Eldest daughter
of the Church\" by the papacy,[^36] and French kings were called \"the
Most Christian Kings of France\".

![With [Clovis](Clovis_I "wikilink")'s conversion to Catholicism in
498, the [Frankish monarchy](List_of_Frankish_kings "wikilink"),
[elective](Elective_monarchy "wikilink") and
[secular](Secular_state "wikilink") until then, became
[hereditary](Hereditary_monarchy "wikilink") and of [divine
right](Divine_right_of_kings "wikilink").](Chlodwigs_taufe.jpg "With Clovis's conversion to Catholicism in 498, the Frankish monarchy, elective and secular until then, became hereditary and of divine right.")
The Franks embraced the Christian [Gallo-Roman
culture](Gallo-Roman_culture "wikilink"), and Gaul was renamed
_[Francia](Francia "wikilink")_ (\"Land of the Franks\"). The Germanic
Franks adopted [Romanic languages](Romance_languages "wikilink"). Clovis
made [Paris](Paris "wikilink") his capital and established the
[Merovingian dynasty](Merovingian_dynasty "wikilink"), but his kingdom
would not survive his death. The Franks treated land as a private
possession and divided it among their heirs, so four kingdoms emerged
from that of Clovis: Paris, [Orléans](Orléans "wikilink"),
[Soissons](Soissons "wikilink"), and [Rheims](Reims "wikilink"). The
[last Merovingian kings](Roi_fainéant "wikilink") [lost
power](Power_behind_the_throne "wikilink") to their [mayors of the
palace](Mayor_of_the_palace "wikilink") (head of household). One mayor
of the palace, [Charles Martel](Charles_Martel "wikilink"), defeated an
[Umayyad](Umayyad_Caliphate "wikilink") invasion at the [Battle of
Tours](Battle_of_Tours "wikilink") in 732.[^37] His son, [Pepin the
Short](Pepin_the_Short "wikilink"), seized the crown of Francia from the
weakened Merovingians and founded the [Carolingian
dynasty](Carolingian_dynasty "wikilink"). Pepin\'s son
[Charlemagne](Charlemagne "wikilink") reunited the Frankish kingdoms and
built an empire across Western and [Central
Europe](Central_Europe "wikilink").

Proclaimed [Holy Roman Emperor](Holy_Roman_Emperor "wikilink") by [Pope
Leo III](Pope_Leo_III "wikilink") and thus establishing the French
government\'s longtime [historical
association](History_of_the_Catholic_Church_in_France "wikilink") with
the [Catholic Church](Catholic_Church "wikilink"),[^38] Charlemagne
tried to revive the [Western Roman
Empire](Western_Roman_Empire "wikilink") and its cultural grandeur.
Charlemagne\'s son [Louis I](Louis_the_Pious "wikilink") kept the empire
united, however in 843 it was divided between Louis\' three sons into
[East Francia](East_Francia "wikilink"), [Middle
Francia](Middle_Francia "wikilink") and [West
Francia](West_Francia "wikilink"). West Francia approximated the area
occupied by modern France and was its precursor.[^39]

Throughout the 9th and 10th centuries, incessant [Viking
raids](Viking_expansion "wikilink") severely weakened the Carolingian
monarchy, accelerating the fragmentation of West Francia into a
decentralised, [feudal society](feudalism "wikilink"). As central
authority faltered, local nobility secured hereditary rights to their
titles and lands, creating a system where the king\'s authority became
more religious and symbolic than secular, frequently challenged by
powerful territorial princes. After the [Battle of
Hastings](Battle_of_Hastings "wikilink") in 1066, [William the
Conqueror](William_the_Conqueror "wikilink") added \"King of England\"
to his titles, becoming vassal and the equal of the king of France,
creating recurring tensions.

### High and Late Middle Ages (10th--15th century) {#high_and_late_middle_ages_10th15th_century}

```{=mediawiki}
{{See also|France in the Middle Ages}}
```

[thumb\|upright=.8\|[Joan of Arc](Joan_of_Arc "wikilink") led the
[French Army](French_Army "wikilink") to several important victories
during the [Hundred Years\' War](Hundred_Years'\_War "wikilink")
(1337--1453), which paved the way for the final
victory.](File:Joan_of_Arc_miniature_graded.jpg "wikilink")

The Carolingian dynasty ruled France until 987, when [Hugh
Capet](Hugh_Capet "wikilink") was crowned [king of the
Franks](List_of_French_monarchs "wikilink").[^40] His descendants
unified the country through wars and inheritance. From 1190, the
[Capetian rulers](Capetian_dynasty "wikilink") began to be referred as
\"kings of France\" rather than \"kings of the Franks\".[^41] Later
kings expanded their directly possessed [_domaine
royal_](Crown_lands_of_France "wikilink") to cover over half of modern
France by the 15th century. Royal authority became more assertive,
centred on a [hierarchically conceived
society](Estates_of_the_realm "wikilink") distinguishing
[nobility](French_nobility "wikilink"), clergy, and
[commoners](<Estates_General_(France)> "wikilink").

The nobility played a prominent role in [Crusades](Crusades "wikilink")
to restore Christian access to the [Holy Land](Holy_Land "wikilink").
French knights made up most reinforcements in the 200 years of the
Crusades, in such a fashion that the Arabs referred to crusaders as
_Franj_.[^42] French Crusaders imported French into the
[Levant](Levant "wikilink"), making [Old French](Old_French "wikilink")
the base of the _[lingua franca](lingua_franca "wikilink")_ (\"Frankish
language\") of the [Crusader states](Crusader_states "wikilink").[^43]
The [Albigensian Crusade](Albigensian_Crusade "wikilink") was launched
in 1209 to eliminate the heretical [Cathars](Catharism "wikilink") in
the southwest of modern-day France.[^44]

From the 11th century, the [House of
Plantagenet](House_of_Plantagenet "wikilink"), rulers of the [County of
Anjou](County_of_Anjou "wikilink"), established its dominion over the
surrounding provinces of [Maine](<Maine_(province)> "wikilink") and
[Touraine](Touraine "wikilink"), then built an \"empire\" from England
to the [Pyrenees](Pyrenees "wikilink"), covering half of modern France.
Tensions between France and the [Plantagenet
empire](Angevin_Empire "wikilink") would last a hundred years, until
[Philip II of France](Philip_II_of_France "wikilink") conquered, between
1202 and 1214, most continental possessions of the empire, leaving
England and [Aquitaine](Aquitaine "wikilink") to the Plantagenets.

[Charles IV the Fair](Charles_IV_of_France "wikilink") died without an
heir in 1328.[^45] The crown passed to [Philip of
Valois](Philip_VI_of_France "wikilink") rather than Edward Plantagenet,
who had become [Edward III](Edward_III "wikilink") of England the
previous year. During the reign of Philip, the monarchy reached the
height of its medieval power.[^46] Edward contested Philip\'s throne in
1337, beginning the intermittent [Hundred Years\'
War](Hundred_Years'_War "wikilink") between England and France.[^47]
Boundaries changed, but landholdings inside France by English kings
remained extensive for decades. With charismatic leaders such as [Joan
of Arc](Joan_of_Arc "wikilink"), French counterattacks won back most
English continental territories. France was struck by the [Black
Death](Black_Death "wikilink"), from which half of the 17 million
population died.[^48]

### Early modern period (15th century--1789) {#early_modern_period_15th_century1789}

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|France in the early modern period}}
```

The [French Renaissance](French_Renaissance "wikilink") saw cultural
development and standardisation of French, which became the [official
language of France](Ordinance_of_Villers-Cotterêts "wikilink") and
Europe\'s aristocracy. France became rivals of the [House of
Habsburg](House_of_Habsburg "wikilink") during the [Italian
Wars](Italian_Wars "wikilink"), which would dictate much of their later
foreign policy until the mid-18th century. French explorers claimed
lands in the Americas, paving expansion of the [French colonial
empire](French_colonial_empire "wikilink"). The rise of Protestantism
led France to a civil war known as the [French Wars of
Religion](French_Wars_of_Religion "wikilink").[^49] This forced
[Huguenots](Huguenots "wikilink") to flee to Protestant regions such as
the [British Isles](British_Isles "wikilink") and
[Switzerland](Switzerland "wikilink"). The wars were ended by [Henri
IV\'s](Henry_IV_of_France "wikilink") [Edict of
Nantes](Edict_of_Nantes "wikilink"), which granted some freedom of
religion to the Huguenots. [Spanish](Habsburg_Spain "wikilink")
troops[^50] assisted the Catholics from 1589 to 1594 and invaded France
in 1597. Spain and France returned to all-out war between 1635 and 1659.
The [Franco-Spanish War](<Franco-Spanish_War_(1635–1659)> "wikilink") cost
France 300,000 casualties; total deaths on both sides were 200,000.[^51]

Under [Louis XIII](Louis_XIII "wikilink"), [Cardinal
Richelieu](Cardinal_Richelieu "wikilink") promoted centralisation of the
state and reinforced royal power. He destroyed castles of defiant lords
and denounced the use of private armies. By the end of the 1620s,
Richelieu established \"the royal monopoly of force\".[^52] France
fought in the [Thirty Years\' War](Thirty_Years'_War "wikilink"),
supporting the Protestant side against the Habsburgs. From the 16th to
the 19th century, France was responsible for about 10% of the
[transatlantic slave trade](Atlantic_slave_trade "wikilink").[^53]

[thumb\|upright=.8\|alt=Louis XIV of France standing in plate armour and
blue sash facing left holding baton\|[Louis XIV](Louis_XIV "wikilink"),
the \"Sun King\", was the [absolute monarch of
France](Absolute_monarchy_in_France "wikilink") who made the country the
[leading power](leading_power "wikilink") in
Europe.](File:Louis_XIV_of_France.jpg "wikilink")

During [Louis XIV](Louis_XIV "wikilink")\'s minority, trouble known as
[The Fronde](The_Fronde "wikilink") occurred. This rebellion was driven
by feudal lords and [sovereign courts](Parliament "wikilink") as a
reaction to the [royal absolute
power](<Absolutism_(European_history)> "wikilink"). The monarchy reached
its peak during the 17th century and reign of Louis XIV, during which
France further increased its influence.[^54] By turning lords into
[courtiers](courtier "wikilink") at the [Palace of
Versailles](Palace_of_Versailles "wikilink"), his command of the
military went unchallenged. The \"Sun King\" made France the leading
European power. France became the [most populous European
country](Demographics_of_France "wikilink") and had tremendous influence
over European politics, economy, and culture. French became the
most-used language in diplomacy,
[science](Languages_of_science "wikilink"), and literature until the
20th century.[^55] France took control of territories in the Americas,
Africa and Asia. In 1685, Louis XIV [revoked the Edict of
Nantes](Edict_of_Fontainebleau "wikilink"), forcing thousands of
Huguenots into exile and published the _[Code
Noir](Code_Noir "wikilink")_ providing the legal framework for slavery
and expelling Jews from French colonies.[^56]

Under the wars of [Louis XV](Louis_XV "wikilink"), France lost [New
France](New_France "wikilink") and most [Indian
possessions](French_India "wikilink") after its defeat in the [Seven
Years\' War](Seven_Years'_War "wikilink") (1756--1763). [Its European
territory](Metropolitan_France "wikilink") kept growing, however, with
acquisitions such as [Lorraine](Lorraine "wikilink") and
[Corsica](Corsica "wikilink"). Louis XV\'s weak rule, including the
decadence of his court, discredited the monarchy, which in part paved
the way for the [French Revolution](French_Revolution "wikilink").[^57]

[Louis XVI](Louis_XVI "wikilink") [supported America with money, fleets
and armies](France_in_the_American_Revolutionary_War "wikilink"),
helping them win [independence from Great
Britain](American_Revolutionary_War "wikilink"). France gained revenge
but verged on bankruptcy---a factor that contributed to the Revolution.
Some of the [Enlightenment](Age_of_Enlightenment "wikilink") occurred in
French intellectual circles, and scientific breakthroughs, such as the
[naming of oxygen](Antoine_Lavoisier "wikilink") and the first [hot air
balloon carrying passengers](Montgolfier_brothers "wikilink"), were
achieved by French scientists. French explorers took part in the
[voyages of scientific
exploration](European_and_American_voyages_of_scientific_exploration "wikilink")
through maritime expeditions. Enlightenment philosophy, in which
[reason](Rationalism "wikilink") is advocated as the primary source of
[legitimacy](Political_legitimacy "wikilink"), undermined the power of
and support for the monarchy and was a factor in the Revolution.

### Revolutionary France (1789--1799) {#revolutionary_france_17891799}

![The [Storming of the Bastille](Storming_of_the_Bastille "wikilink") on
14 July 1789 was the most emblematic event of the [French
Revolution](French_Revolution "wikilink").](Prise_de_la_Bastille.jpg "The Storming of the Bastille on 14 July 1789 was the most emblematic event of the French Revolution.")
The French Revolution was a period of political and societal change that
began with the [Estates General of
1789](Estates_General_of_1789 "wikilink"), and ended with the [coup of
18 Brumaire](coup_of_18_Brumaire "wikilink") in 1799 and the formation
of the [French Consulate](French_Consulate "wikilink"). Many of its
ideas are fundamental principles of [liberal
democracy](liberal_democracy "wikilink"),[^58] while its values and
institutions remain central to modern political discourse.[^59]

[Its causes](Causes_of_the_French_Revolution "wikilink") were a
combination of social, political and economic factors, which the
`{{lang|fr|[[Ancien Régime]]}}`{=mediawiki} proved unable to manage. A
financial crisis and social distress led in May 1789 to the
[convocation](convocation "wikilink") of the [Estates
General](Estates_General_of_1789 "wikilink"), which was converted into a
[National Assembly](<National_Assembly_(French_Revolution)> "wikilink") in
June. The [Storming of the
Bastille](Storming_of_the_Bastille "wikilink") on 14 July led to a
series of radical measures by the Assembly, among them the [abolition of
feudalism](Abolition_of_feudalism_in_France "wikilink"), state control
over the [Catholic Church in
France](Catholic_Church_in_France "wikilink"), and a [declaration of
rights](Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen "wikilink").[^60]

The next three years were dominated by a struggle for political control,
exacerbated by [economic depression](economic_depression "wikilink").
Military defeats following the outbreak of the [French Revolutionary
Wars](French_Revolutionary_Wars "wikilink") in April 1792 resulted in
the [insurrection of 10 August
1792](insurrection_of_10_August_1792 "wikilink"). The [monarchy was
abolished](Proclamation_of_the_abolition_of_the_monarchy "wikilink") and
replaced by the [French First
Republic](French_First_Republic "wikilink") in September, while [Louis
XVI was executed](execution_of_Louis_XVI "wikilink") in January 1793.[^61]

After another [revolt in June
1793](Insurrection_of_31_May_–_2_June_1793 "wikilink"), the constitution
was suspended and power passed from the [National
Convention](National_Convention "wikilink") to the [Committee of Public
Safety](Committee_of_Public_Safety "wikilink"). About 16,000 people were
executed in a [Reign of Terror](Reign_of_Terror "wikilink"), which
[ended in July 1794](Thermidorian_Reaction "wikilink"). Weakened by
external threats and internal opposition, the Republic was replaced in
1795 by the [Directory](French_Directory "wikilink"). Four years later
in 1799, the [Consulate](French_Consulate "wikilink") seized power in [a
coup](Coup_of_18_Brumaire "wikilink") led by
[Napoleon](Napoleon "wikilink").[^62]

### Napoleonic France (1799--1815) {#napoleonic_france_17991815}

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|France in the long nineteenth century}}
```

![[Napoleon](Napoleon "wikilink"), [Emperor of the
French](Emperor_of_the_French "wikilink"), built [a vast empire across
Europe](First_French_Empire "wikilink").[^63]](Jacques-Louis_David_-_The_Emperor_Napoleon_in_His_Study_at_the_Tuileries_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg "Napoleon, Emperor of the French, built a vast empire across Europe.")
[Napoleon](Napoleon "wikilink") became [First
Consul](French_Consulate "wikilink") in 1799 and later
[Emperor](Constitution_of_the_Year_XII "wikilink") of the [French
Empire](First_French_Empire "wikilink"). Changing sets of [European
coalitions](French_Revolutionary_and_Napoleonic_Wars "wikilink")
declared [wars on Napoleon\'s empire](Napoleonic_Wars "wikilink"). His
armies conquered most of continental Europe with swift victories such as
the [battles of Jena-Auerstadt](Battle_of_Jena–Auerstedt "wikilink") and
[Austerlitz](Battle_of_Austerlitz "wikilink"). Members of the
[Bonaparte](House_of_Bonaparte "wikilink") family were appointed
monarchs in some of the newly established kingdoms.[^65]

These victories led to the worldwide expansion of French revolutionary
ideals and reforms, such as the [metric
system](metric_system "wikilink"), [Napoleonic
Code](Napoleonic_Code "wikilink") and [Declaration of the Rights of
Man](Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen "wikilink"). In
1812 Napoleon [attacked Russia](French_invasion_of_Russia "wikilink"),
reaching [Moscow](Moscow "wikilink"). Thereafter his army disintegrated
through supply problems, disease, Russian attacks, and finally winter.
After this catastrophic campaign and the ensuing [uprising of European
monarchies](War_of_the_Sixth_Coalition "wikilink") against his rule,
Napoleon was defeated. About a million Frenchmen [died during the
Napoleonic Wars](Napoleonic_Wars_casualties "wikilink"), including
306,000 killed.[^66][^67] After his [brief
return](Hundred_Days "wikilink") from exile, Napoleon was finally
defeated in 1815 at the [Battle of
Waterloo](Battle_of_Waterloo "wikilink"), and the [Bourbon monarchy was
restored](Bourbon_Restoration_in_France "wikilink") with new
constitutional limitations.

### Colonial empire {#colonial_empire}

The discredited Bourbon dynasty was overthrown by the [July
Revolution](July_Revolution "wikilink") of 1830, which established the
constitutional [July Monarchy](July_Monarchy "wikilink"); French troops
began the [conquest of Algeria](French_conquest_of_Algeria "wikilink").
Unrest led to the [French Revolution of
1848](French_Revolution_of_1848 "wikilink") and the end of the July
Monarchy. The abolition of slavery and the introduction of male
universal suffrage was re-enacted in 1848. In 1852, president of the
French Republic, [Louis-Napoléon Bonaparte](Napoleon_III "wikilink"),
Napoleon I\'s nephew, was proclaimed emperor of the [Second
Empire](Second_French_Empire "wikilink"), as Napoleon III. He multiplied
French interventions abroad, especially in
[Crimea](Crimean_War "wikilink"),
[Mexico](Second_French_intervention_in_Mexico "wikilink") and
[Italy](Second_Italian_War_of_Independence "wikilink"). Napoleon III was
unseated following defeat in the [Franco-Prussian
War](Franco-Prussian_War "wikilink") of 1870, and his regime was
replaced by the [Third Republic](French_Third_Republic "wikilink"). By
1875, the French conquest of Algeria was complete, with approximately
825,000 Algerians killed from famine, disease, and violence.[^68] France
suffered an estimated 10,000 killed and 35,000 wounded across all
colonial campaigns.[^69] A few thousand died in Mexico or
[Vietnam](French_conquest_of_Vietnam "wikilink"), but the vast majority
perished in Algeria. Disease claimed an even heavier toll; one estimate
puts total French and [Foreign Legion](French_Foreign_Legion "wikilink")
deaths from battle and disease for the entire 19th century at
110,000.[^70]

![[French colonial empires](French_colonial_empire "wikilink"):
`{{legend|#118cb4|First}}`{=mediawiki}
`{{legend|#002c74|Second}}`{=mediawiki}](EmpireFrench.png "French colonial empires:  ")

France had [colonial possessions](French_colonial_empire "wikilink")
since the beginning of the 17th century, but in the 19th and 20th
centuries its empire extended significantly and became [the
second-largest](List_of_largest_empires "wikilink") behind the [British
Empire](British_Empire "wikilink").[^71] Including metropolitan France,
the total area reached almost 13 million square kilometres in the 1920s
and 1930s, 9% of the world\'s land. Known as the *[Belle
Époque](Belle*Époque "wikilink")\_, the turn of the century was
characterised by optimism, regional peace, economic prosperity and
technological, scientific and cultural innovations. In 1905, [state
secularism](Secular_state "wikilink") was [officially
established](1905_French_law_on_the_Separation_of_the_Churches_and_the_State "wikilink").

### Early to mid-20th century (1914--1946) {#early_to_mid_20th_century_19141946}

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|History of France (1900–present)}}
```

![French [Poilus](Poilu "wikilink") posing with their war-torn flag in
1917, during World War
I](El_114_de_infantería,_en_París,_el_14_de_julio_de_1917,_León_Gimpel.jpg "French Poilus posing with their war-torn flag in 1917, during World War I")
France was [invaded by Germany and defended by Great
Britain](French_entry_into_World_War_I "wikilink") at the start of
[World War I](World_War_I "wikilink") in August 1914. A rich industrial
area in the north was occupied. France and the
[Allies](Allies_of_World_War_I "wikilink") emerged victorious against
the [Central Powers](Central_Powers "wikilink") at tremendous human
cost. It left 1.4 million French soldiers dead, 4% of its
population.[^72][^73] In addition, France suffered 4,266,000
wounded.[^74] Interwar was marked by [intense international
tensions](Events_preceding_World_War_II_in_Europe "wikilink") and social
reforms introduced by the [Popular Front
government](<Popular_Front_(France)> "wikilink") (e.g., [annual
leave](annual_leave "wikilink"), [eight-hour
workdays](Eight-hour_day "wikilink"), [women in
government](women_in_government "wikilink")).

In 1940, France was [invaded and quickly
defeated](Battle_of_France "wikilink") by [Nazi
Germany](Nazi_Germany "wikilink"). France was divided into a [German
occupation
zone](German_military_administration_in_occupied_France_during_World_War_II "wikilink")
in the north, an [Italian occupation
zone](Italian_occupation_of_France "wikilink") and an unoccupied
territory, the rest of France, which consisted of southern France and
the French empire. The [Vichy government](Vichy_France "wikilink"), an
authoritarian regime collaborating with Germany, ruled the unoccupied
territory. [Free France](Free_France "wikilink"), the
government-in-exile led by[Charles de
Gaulle](Charles_de_Gaulle "wikilink"), was set up in London.[^75]

From 1942 to 1944, about 160,000 French citizens, including around
[75,000 Jews](The_Holocaust_in_France "wikilink"),[^76] were deported to
[death](Extermination_camp "wikilink") and [concentration
camps](Internment "wikilink").[^77] On 6 June 1944, the Allies [invaded
Normandy](Operation_Overlord "wikilink"), and in August they [invaded
Provence](Operation_Dragoon "wikilink"). The Allies and [French
Resistance](French_Resistance "wikilink") emerged victorious, and French
sovereignty was restored with the [Provisional Government of the French
Republic](Provisional_Government_of_the_French_Republic "wikilink")
(GPRF). This interim government, established by de Gaulle, continued to
[wage war against
Germany](Western_Allied_invasion_of_Germany "wikilink") and to [purge
collaborators from office](Épuration_légale "wikilink"). It made
important reforms e.g. suffrage extended to women and the creation of a
[social security](Social_security_in_France "wikilink") system.

### 1946--present

[thumb\|upright=.8\|alt=Charles de Gaulle seated in uniform looking left
with folded arms\|[Charles de Gaulle](Charles_de_Gaulle "wikilink"), a
hero of World War I, leader of the [Free
French](Free_French_Forces "wikilink") during [World War
II](World_War_II "wikilink"), and [President of
France](President_of_France "wikilink")](File:De_Gaulle-OWI.jpg "wikilink")
A new constitution resulted in the [Fourth
Republic](French_Fourth_Republic "wikilink") (1946--1958), which saw
strong economic growth (_les [Trente
Glorieuses](Trente_Glorieuses "wikilink")_). France was a founding
member of [NATO](NATO "wikilink") and attempted to [regain control of
French Indochina](First_Indochina_War "wikilink"), but was defeated by
the [Viet Minh](Viet_Minh "wikilink") in 1954. France faced another
[anti-colonialist](anti-colonialist "wikilink") [conflict in
Algeria](Algerian_War "wikilink"), then part of France and home to over
one million European settlers ([Pied-Noir](Pied-Noir "wikilink")). The
French systematically used torture and repression, including
extrajudicial killings to keep control.[^78] This conflict nearly led to
a coup and civil war.[^79]

During the [May 1958 crisis](May_1958_crisis_in_France "wikilink"), the
weak Fourth Republic gave way to the [Fifth
Republic](French_Fifth_Republic "wikilink"), which included a
strengthened presidency.[^80] The war concluded with the [Évian
Accords](Évian_Accords "wikilink") in 1962 which led to [Algerian
independence](1962_Algerian_independence_referendum "wikilink"), at a
high price: between half a million and one million deaths and over
2 million internally-displaced Algerians.[^81] Around one million
Pied-Noirs and [Harkis](Harki "wikilink") fled from Algeria to
France.[^82] A vestige of the empire is the [French overseas departments
and territories](Overseas_France "wikilink").

During the [Cold War](Cold_War "wikilink"), de Gaulle pursued a policy
of \"national independence\" towards the
[Western](Western_Bloc "wikilink") and [Eastern
blocs](Eastern_Bloc "wikilink"). He withdrew from NATO\'s
military-integrated command (while remaining within the alliance),
launched a [nuclear development
programme](Force_de_dissuasion "wikilink") and made France the [fourth
nuclear power](France_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction "wikilink"). He
[restored](Élysée_Treaty "wikilink") cordial [Franco-German
relations](France–Germany_relations "wikilink") to create a European
counterweight between American and Soviet spheres of influence. However,
he opposed any development of a [supranational
Europe](Supranational_union "wikilink"), favouring [sovereign
nations](Sovereign_state "wikilink"). The revolt of [May
1968](May_68 "wikilink") had an enormous social impact; it was a
watershed moment when a conservative moral ideal (religion, patriotism,
respect for authority) shifted to a more liberal moral ideal
(secularism, individualism, sexual revolution). Although the revolt was
a political failure (the [Gaullist](Gaullism "wikilink") party emerged
stronger than before) it announced a split between the French and de
Gaulle, who resigned.[^83]

In the post-Gaullist era, France remained one of the most developed
[economies in the world](<List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal)> "wikilink")
but faced crises that resulted in high unemployment rates and increasing
public debt. In the late 20th and early 21st centuries, France has been
at the forefront of the development of a supranational [European
Union](European_Union "wikilink"), notably by signing the [Maastricht
Treaty](Maastricht_Treaty "wikilink") in 1992, establishing the
[eurozone](eurozone "wikilink") in 1999[^84] and signing the [Treaty of
Lisbon](Treaty_of_Lisbon "wikilink") in 2007.[^85] France has fully
reintegrated into NATO and since participated in most NATO-sponsored
wars.[^86] ![[Calais
Jungle](Calais_Jungle "wikilink")](Overview_of_Calais_Jungle.jpg "Calais Jungle")
Since the 19th century, France has [received many
immigrants](Immigration_to_France "wikilink"), often male [foreign
workers](foreign_worker "wikilink") from European Catholic countries who
generally returned home when not employed.[^87] During the 1970s France
faced an economic crisis and allowed new immigrants (mostly from the
[Maghreb](Maghreb "wikilink"), in northwest Africa)[^88] to permanently
[settle in France with their families](Family_reunification "wikilink")
and acquire citizenship. It resulted in hundreds of thousands of Muslims
living in subsidised public housing and suffering from high unemployment
rates.[^89] The government had a policy of
[assimilation](Cultural_assimilation "wikilink") of immigrants, where
they were expected to adhere to French values and norms.[^90] From the
2000s, France experienced continued immigration from Africa, the Middle
East, and Asia. In addition, [Calais](Migrants_around_Calais "wikilink")
became a transit area for migrants.

Since the [1995 public transport
bombings](1995_France_bombings "wikilink"), France has been targeted by
Islamist organisations, notably the [_Charlie Hebdo_
attack](January_2015_Île-de-France_attacks "wikilink") in 2015 which
provoked the [largest public rallies](Republican_marches "wikilink") in
French history, gathering 4.4 million people,[^91] the [November 2015
Paris attacks](November_2015_Paris_attacks "wikilink") which resulted in
130 deaths, the deadliest attack on French soil since World War II[^92]
and the deadliest in the European Union since the [Madrid train bombings
in 2004](2004_Madrid_train_bombings "wikilink").[^93] [Opération
Chammal](Opération_Chammal "wikilink"), France\'s military efforts to
contain [ISIS](Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant "wikilink"), killed
over 1,000 ISIS troops between 2014 and 2015.[^94]

## Geography

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Geography of France}}
```

![[Chamonix](Chamonix "wikilink") valley with the [Mont
Blanc](Mont_Blanc "wikilink") at background, the highest mountain in the
[Alps](Alps "wikilink") and [Western Europe](Western_Europe "wikilink")
on the border with
[Italy](Italy "wikilink")](Chamonix_valley_from_la_Flégère,2010_07.JPG "Chamonix valley with the Mont Blanc at background, the highest mountain in the Alps and Western Europe on the border with Italy")
The vast majority of France\'s territory and population is situated in
Western Europe and is called [Metropolitan
France](Metropolitan_France "wikilink"). It is bordered by the [North
Sea](North_Sea "wikilink") in the north, the [English
Channel](English_Channel "wikilink") in the northwest, the [Atlantic
Ocean](Atlantic_Ocean "wikilink") in the west and the [Mediterranean
Sea](Mediterranean_Sea "wikilink") in the southeast. Its land borders
consist of [Belgium](Belgium "wikilink") and
[Luxembourg](Luxembourg "wikilink") in the northeast,
[Germany](Germany "wikilink") and [Switzerland](Switzerland "wikilink")
in the east, [Italy](Italy "wikilink") and [Monaco](Monaco "wikilink")
in the southeast, and [Andorra](Andorra "wikilink") and
[Spain](Spain "wikilink") in the south and southwest. Except for the
northeast, most of France\'s land borders are roughly delineated by
natural boundaries and geographic features: to the south, the
[Pyrenees](Pyrenees "wikilink") and the [Alps](Alps "wikilink"); to the
southeast, [Jura Mountains](Jura_Mountains "wikilink"); and to the east,
the [Rhine](Rhine "wikilink") river. Including [Overseas
France](Overseas_France "wikilink"), it also borders
[Suriname](Suriname "wikilink") and [Brazil](Brazil "wikilink") through
[French Guiana](French_Guiana "wikilink") in [South
America](South_America "wikilink"), and the
[Netherlands](Kingdom_of_the_Netherlands "wikilink") through [Saint
Martin](<Saint_Martin_(island)> "wikilink") in the
[Caribbean](Caribbean "wikilink").[^95] Metropolitan France includes
various coastal islands, of which the largest is
[Corsica](Corsica "wikilink"). Metropolitan France is situated mostly
between latitudes [41°](41st_parallel_north "wikilink") and [51°
N](51st_parallel_north "wikilink"), and longitudes [6°
W](6th_meridian_west "wikilink") and [10°
E](10th_meridian_east "wikilink"), on the western edge of Europe, and
thus lies within the northern [temperate](Temperate_climate "wikilink")
zone. Its continental part covers about 1000 km from north to south and
from east to west.

Metropolitan France covers
`{{Convert|551500|km2|sqmi|0}}`{=mediawiki},[^96] the largest among
[European Union](European_Union "wikilink") members.[^97] France\'s
total land area, with its overseas departments and territories
(excluding [Adélie Land](Adélie_Land "wikilink")), is
`{{Convert|643801|km2|sqmi|0|abbr=on}}`{=mediawiki},[^98] 0.45% of the
total land area on Earth. France possesses a wide variety of landscapes,
from coastal plains in the north and west to the mountain ranges of the
Alps in the southeast, the [Massif Central](Massif_Central "wikilink")
in the south-central and [Pyrenees](Pyrenees "wikilink") in the
southwest. With numerous [overseas departments and
territories](Overseas_France "wikilink") scattered across the planet,
France possesses the second-largest [exclusive economic
zone](exclusive_economic_zone "wikilink") (EEZ) in the world, covering
`{{Convert|11035000|km2|sqmi|-3|abbr=on}}`{=mediawiki}. Its EEZ covers
approximately 8% of the total surface of all the EEZs of the world.

### Geology, topography and hydrography {#geology_topography_and_hydrography}

![Geological formations near [Roussillon,
Vaucluse](Roussillon,_Vaucluse "wikilink")](2013.07.05_roussillon_-_roque_anthéron_172.JPG "Geological formations near Roussillon, Vaucluse")
Metropolitan France has a wide variety of topographic sets and natural
landscapes. During the [Hercynian uplift](Hercynian_uplift "wikilink")
in the [Paleozoic](Paleozoic "wikilink") Era, the [Armorican
Massif](Armorican_Massif "wikilink"), the Massif Central, the
[Morvan](Morvan "wikilink"), the [Vosges](Vosges "wikilink") and
[Ardennes](Ardennes "wikilink") ranges and the island of Corsica were
formed. These massifs delineate several sedimentary basins such as the
[Aquitaine Basin](Aquitaine_Basin "wikilink") in the southwest and the
[Paris Basin](Paris_Basin "wikilink") in the north. Various routes of
natural passage, such as the [Rhône Valley](Rhône_Valley "wikilink"),
allow easy communication. The Alpine, Pyrenean and Jura Mountains are
much younger and have less eroded forms. At
`{{Convert|4810.45|m|ft|0}}`{=mediawiki}[^99] above sea level, [Mont
Blanc](Mont_Blanc "wikilink") in the Alps is the highest point in
Western Europe. 60% of municipalities are classified as having moderate
seismic risk.

The coastlines offer contrasting landscapes: mountain ranges along the
[French Riviera](French_Riviera "wikilink"), coastal cliffs such as the
[Côte d\'Albâtre](Côte_d'Albâtre "wikilink"), and wide sandy plains in
the [Languedoc](Languedoc "wikilink"). Corsica lies off the
Mediterranean coast. France has an extensive river system consisting of
the four major rivers: the [Seine](Seine "wikilink"), the
[Loire](Loire "wikilink"), the [Garonne](Garonne "wikilink"), the
[Rhône](Rhône "wikilink") and their tributaries, whose combined
catchment includes over 62% of the metropolitan territory. The Rhône
divides the Massif Central from the Alps and flows into the
Mediterranean Sea at the [Camargue](Camargue "wikilink"). The Garonne
meets the [Dordogne](<Dordogne_(river)> "wikilink") just after
[Bordeaux](Bordeaux "wikilink"), forming the [Gironde
estuary](Gironde_estuary "wikilink"), the largest estuary in Western
Europe which after approximately `{{Convert|100|km|mi|0}}`{=mediawiki}
empties into the Atlantic Ocean.[^100] Other water courses drain towards
the [Meuse](Meuse "wikilink") and Rhine along the northeastern borders.
France has `{{Cvt|11000000|km2}}`{=mediawiki} of marine waters within
three oceans under its jurisdiction, of which 97% are overseas.

### Environment

```{=mediawiki}
{{See also|List of national parks of France|Regional natural parks of France|Climate change in France}}
```

On June 28, 2019, in [Vérargues](Vérargues "wikilink"), the temperature
reached 46 °C, setting a temperature record in Metropolitan France since
records began.[^101] [Climate
change](Climate_change_in_France "wikilink") means that this kind of
heatwave in France can be expected to occur 10 times more frequently
than it would have done a century ago.[^102][^103] France was one of the
first countries to create an environment ministry, in 1971.[^104] France
is ranked [19th by carbon dioxide
emissions](List_of_countries_by_carbon_dioxide_emissions "wikilink") due
to the country\'s heavy investment in [nuclear
power](Nuclear_power_in_France "wikilink") following the [1973 oil
crisis](1973_oil_crisis "wikilink"),[^105] which now accounts for 75 per
cent of its electricity production[^106] and results in less
pollution.[^107][^108] According to the 2020 [Environmental Performance
Index](Environmental_Performance_Index "wikilink") conducted by
[Yale](Yale "wikilink") and [Columbia](Columbia_University "wikilink"),
France was the fifth most environmentally conscious country in the
world.[^109][^110] Like all European Union state members, France agreed
to cut carbon emissions by at least 20% of 1990 levels by 2020.[^111]
`{{As of|2009}}`{=mediawiki}, carbon dioxide emissions per capita are
lower than that of China.[^112] The country was set to impose a [carbon
tax](carbon_tax "wikilink") in 2009;[^113] however, the plan was
abandoned due to fears of it burdening French businesses.[^114]

![The [Gironde estuary](Gironde_estuary "wikilink") is classified as a
[marine nature
park](Gironde_estuary_and_Pertuis_sea_Marine_Nature_Park "wikilink") and
is part of the [Natura 2000](Natura_2000 "wikilink") network. The left
bank is part of the [Médoc](Médoc "wikilink") [regional nature
park](Regional_nature_parks_of_France "wikilink"). With a surface area
of 635 km^2^ (245 sq mi), it is the largest estuary in
Europe.](Roselière_de_Pauillac_et_carrelets_sur_l'estuaire_de_la_Gironde,_France.jpg "The Gironde estuary is classified as a marine nature park and is part of the Natura 2000 network. The left bank is part of the Médoc regional nature park. With a surface area of 635 km2 (245 sq mi), it is the largest estuary in Europe.")
Forests account for 31 per cent of the land area---the fourth-highest
proportion in Europe---representing an increase of 7 per cent since 1990.[^115][^116][^117] The forests are some of the most diverse in
Europe, comprising more than 140 species of trees.[^118] France had a
2018 [Forest Landscape Integrity
Index](Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index "wikilink") mean score of
4.52/10, ranking it 123rd globally.[^119] There are nine [national
parks](national_park "wikilink")[^120] and 46 [natural
parks](Protected_area "wikilink").[^121] A regional nature park[^122]
(`{{Langx|fr|parc naturel régional|links=no}}`{=mediawiki} or PNR) is a
public establishment in France between local authorities and the
national government covering an inhabited rural area of outstanding
beauty, to protect the scenery and heritage as well as setting up
sustainable economic development in the area.[^123][^124]
`{{As of|2019}}`{=mediawiki} there are 54 PNRs in France.[^125]

## Politics

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Politics of France}}
```

France is a [representative
democracy](representative_democracy "wikilink") organised as a unitary
[semi-presidential
republic](semi-presidential_republic "wikilink").[^126] Democratic
traditions and values are deeply rooted in French culture, identity and
politics.[^127] The [Constitution of the Fifth
Republic](Constitution_of_France "wikilink") was approved by [referendum
in 1958](1958_French_constitutional_referendum "wikilink"), establishing
a framework consisting of executive, legislative and judicial
branches.[^128] It sought to address the instability of the Third and
Fourth Republics by combining elements of both the parliamentary and
presidential systems, while greatly strengthening the authority of the
executive relative to the legislature.[^129]

### Government

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Government of France}}
```

```{=mediawiki}
{{Multiple image
| direction     = horizontal
| align         = right
| caption_align = center
| total_width   = 320
| image1        = Emmanuel Macron portrait (cropped).jpg
| image2        = Portrait officiel de Sébastien Lecornu - Ministre des Armées (cropped 2).jpg
| caption1      = [[Emmanuel Macron]]<br /><small>[[President of France|President]]</small>
| caption2      = [[Sébastien Lecornu]]<br /><small>[[Prime Minister of France|Prime Minister]]</small>
| alt1          =
| alt2          =
}}
```

The executive branch has two leaders. The
[president](President_of_France "wikilink"), who is [Emmanuel
Macron](Emmanuel_Macron "wikilink") since 2017, is the [head of
state](head_of_state "wikilink"), elected directly by [universal adult
suffrage](Universal_suffrage "wikilink") for a five-year term.[^130] The
[prime minister](Prime_Minister_of_France "wikilink"), who is [Sébastien
Lecornu](Sébastien_Lecornu "wikilink") since 2025, is the [head of
government](head_of_government "wikilink"), appointed by the president
to lead the government. The president has the power to dissolve
[parliament](French_Parliament "wikilink") or circumvent it by
submitting referendums directly to the people; the president also
appoints judges and civil servants, negotiates and ratifies
international agreements, and serves as
[commander-in-chief](commander-in-chief "wikilink") of the [French Armed
Forces](French_Armed_Forces "wikilink"). The prime minister determines
public policy and oversees the civil service, with an emphasis on
domestic matters.[^131] In the [2022 presidential
election](2022_French_presidential_election "wikilink"), Macron was
re-elected.[^132] Two months later in the [legislative
elections](2022_French_legislative_election "wikilink"), Macron lost his
parliamentary majority and had to form a [minority
government](minority_government "wikilink").[^133]![[Palais
Bourbon](Palais_Bourbon "wikilink") is the meeting place of the
[National Assembly](National_Assembly_(France) "wikilink"), the lower
legislative chamber of the [French
Parliament](French_Parliament "wikilink").](Façade_Palais_Bourbon_3.jpg "Palais Bourbon is the meeting place of the National Assembly, the lower legislative chamber of the French Parliament.")
The legislature consists of the French Parliament, a
[bicameral](Bicameralism "wikilink") body made up of a [lower
house](lower_house "wikilink"), the [National
Assembly](<National_Assembly_(France)> "wikilink") and an [upper
house](upper_house "wikilink"), the
[Senate](<Senate_(France)> "wikilink").[^134] Legislators in the National
Assembly, known as _[députés](<Deputy_(France)> "wikilink")\_, represent
local constituencies and are [directly
elected](Direct_election "wikilink") for five-year terms.[^135] The
Assembly has the power to dismiss the government by majority vote.
Senators are chosen by an [electoral
college](electoral_college "wikilink") for six-year terms, with half the
seats submitted to election every three years.[^136] The Senate\'s
legislative powers are limited; in the event of disagreement between the
two chambers, the National Assembly has the final say.[^137] The
parliament is responsible for determining the rules and principles
concerning most areas of law, political amnesty, and fiscal policy;
however, the government may draft specific details concerning most laws.

From World War II until 2017, French politics was dominated by two
politically opposed groupings: one left-wing, the [French Section of the
Workers\'
International](French_Section_of_the_Workers'_International "wikilink"),
which was succeeded by the [Socialist
Party](<Socialist_Party_(France)> "wikilink") in 1969; and the other
right-wing, the [Gaullist Party](Gaullism "wikilink"), whose name
changed over time to the [Rally of the French
People](Rally_of_the_French_People "wikilink") (1947), the [Union of
Democrats for the
Republic](Union_of_Democrats_for_the_Republic "wikilink") (1958), the
[Rally for the Republic](Rally_for_the_Republic "wikilink") (1976), the
[Union for a Popular Movement](Union_for_a_Popular_Movement "wikilink")
(2007) and [The Republicans](<The_Republicans_(France)> "wikilink") (since
2015). In the [2017
presidential](2017_French_presidential_election "wikilink") and
[legislative elections](2017_French_legislative_election "wikilink"),
the [radical centrist](Radical_centrism "wikilink") party [La République
En Marche!](La_République_En_Marche! "wikilink") (LREM) became the
dominant force, overtaking both Socialists and Republicans. LREM\'s
opponent in the second round of the 2017 and 2022 presidential elections
was the growing far-right party [National
Rally](National_Rally "wikilink") (RN). Since 2020, [The
Ecologists](The_Ecologists "wikilink") have performed well in mayoral
elections in major cities[^138] while on a national level, the [New
Ecological and Social People\'s
Union](New_Ecological_and_Social_People's_Union "wikilink") was the
second-largest voting block elected to the lower house in 2022.[^139]
[Right-wing populist](right-wing_populism "wikilink") RN became the
largest opposition party in the National Assembly in 2022.[^140]

The electorate is constitutionally empowered to vote on amendments
passed by the Parliament and bills submitted by the president.
Referendums have played a key role in shaping French politics and even
foreign policy; voters have decided on such matters as Algeria\'s
independence, the election of the president by popular vote, the
formation of the EU, and the reduction of presidential term
limits.[^141]

### Administrative divisions {#administrative_divisions}

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Administrative divisions of France}}
```

France is divided into 18 [regions](Regions_of_France "wikilink")
(located in Europe and overseas), five [overseas
collectivities](Overseas_collectivity "wikilink") ([French
Polynesia](French_Polynesia "wikilink"), [Saint Pierre and
Miquelon](Saint_Pierre_and_Miquelon "wikilink"), [Wallis and
Futuna](Wallis_and_Futuna "wikilink"), [Saint
Barthélemy](Saint_Barthélemy "wikilink"), and [Saint
Martin](Collectivity_of_Saint_Martin "wikilink")), one [overseas
territory](<Overseas_territory_(France)> "wikilink") ([French Southern and
Antarctic Lands](French_Southern_and_Antarctic_Lands "wikilink")), one
special collectivity ([New Caledonia](New_Caledonia "wikilink")), and
one uninhabited island ([Clipperton](Clipperton_Island "wikilink"))
under the authority of the [Minister of Overseas
France](Minister_of_the_Overseas "wikilink").

#### Regions

```{=mediawiki}
{{France Regions Labelled Map}}
```

Since 2016, France is divided into 18 administrative regions: 13 regions
in metropolitan France (including Corsica),[^142] and five
overseas.[^143] The regions are further subdivided into 101
[departments](Departments_of_France "wikilink"),[^144] which are
numbered mainly alphabetically. The department number is used in postal
codes and was formerly used on [vehicle registration
plates](Vehicle_registration_plates_of_France "wikilink"). Among the 101
French departments, five ([French Guiana](French_Guiana "wikilink"),
[Guadeloupe](Guadeloupe "wikilink"),
[Martinique](Martinique "wikilink"), [Mayotte](Mayotte "wikilink"), and
[Réunion](Réunion "wikilink")) are in overseas regions that are
simultaneously overseas departments, enjoying the same status as
metropolitan departments and are thereby included in the European Union.

The 101 departments are subdivided into 335
[arrondissements](Arrondissements_of_France "wikilink"), which are, in
turn, subdivided into 2,054
[cantons](Cantons_of_France "wikilink").[^145] These cantons are divided
into 36,658 [communes](Communes_of_France "wikilink"), which are
municipalities with an elected municipal council.[^146] Three
communes---Paris, Lyon and Marseille---are subdivided into 45 [municipal
arrondissements](Municipal_arrondissements_of_France "wikilink").

#### Overseas territories and collectivities {#overseas_territories_and_collectivities}

In addition to the 18 regions and 101 departments, the French Republic
has five overseas collectivities ([French
Polynesia](French_Polynesia "wikilink"), [Saint
Barthélemy](Saint_Barthélemy "wikilink"), [Saint
Martin](Collectivity_of_Saint_Martin "wikilink"), [Saint Pierre and
Miquelon](Saint_Pierre_and_Miquelon "wikilink"), and [Wallis and
Futuna](Wallis_and_Futuna "wikilink")), one _[sui
generis](sui_generis "wikilink")_ collectivity ([New
Caledonia](New_Caledonia "wikilink")), one [overseas
territory](<overseas_territory_(France)> "wikilink") ([French Southern and
Antarctic Lands](French_Southern_and_Antarctic_Lands "wikilink")), and
one island possession in the Pacific Ocean (Clipperton Island). Overseas
collectivities and territories form part of the French Republic but do
not form part of the European Union or its fiscal area (except for Saint
Barthélemy, which seceded from Guadeloupe in 2007). The Pacific
Collectivities of French Polynesia, Wallis and Futuna, and New Caledonia
continue to use the [CFP franc](CFP_franc "wikilink")[^147] whose value
is strictly linked to the euro. The five overseas regions use the
euro.[^148]

### Foreign relations {#foreign_relations}

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Foreign relations of France}}
```

France is a founding member of the United Nations and serves as one of
the [permanent members of the UN Security
Council](Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council "wikilink")
with veto rights.[^149] In 2015 it was described as \"the best networked
state in the world\" due to its membership in more international
institutions than any other country;[^150] these include the G7, [World
Trade Organization](World_Trade_Organization "wikilink"),[^151] the
[Pacific Community](Pacific_Community "wikilink")[^152] and the [Indian
Ocean Commission](Indian_Ocean_Commission "wikilink").[^153] It is an
associate member of the [Association of Caribbean
States](Association_of_Caribbean_States "wikilink")[^154] and a leading
member of the
`{{Lang|fr|[[Organisation internationale de la Francophonie]]|italic=no}}`{=mediawiki}
of 84 French-speaking countries.[^155] ![88 states and governments are
part of *[La Francophonie](La_Francophonie "wikilink")*,[^156] which
promotes values of [democracy](democracy "wikilink"),
[multilingualism](multilingualism "wikilink") and [cultural
diversity](cultural_diversity "wikilink").[^157] France has been a key
member of this global organisation since its inception in
1970.](La_francophonie_mapa.svg "88 states and governments are part of La Francophonie, which promotes values of democracy, multilingualism and cultural diversity. France has been a key member of this global organisation since its inception in 1970.")
![The [European Parliament](European_Parliament "wikilink") in
[Strasbourg](Strasbourg "wikilink") (near the border with Germany).
France is a founding member of all EU
institutions.](Inauguration_EYE2014_Parlement_européen_Strasbourg_9_mai_2014.jpg "The European Parliament in Strasbourg (near the border with Germany). France is a founding member of all EU institutions.")

As a significant hub for international relations, France has the
[third-largest
assembly](List_of_diplomatic_missions_of_France "wikilink") of
[diplomatic missions](diplomatic_mission "wikilink"), behind China and
the United States. It also hosts the headquarters of several
[international organisations](international_organization "wikilink"),
including the
[OECD](Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development "wikilink"),
[UNESCO](UNESCO "wikilink"), [Interpol](Interpol "wikilink"), the
[International Bureau of Weights and
Measures](International_Bureau_of_Weights_and_Measures "wikilink"), and
the OIF.[^160]

French foreign policy after World War II has been largely shaped by
membership in the European Union, of which it was a [founding
member](Inner_Six "wikilink"). Since the 1963 [Élysée
Treaty](Élysée_Treaty "wikilink"), France [has developed close
ties](France–Germany_relations "wikilink") with reunified Germany to
become the most influential driving force of the EU.[^161] Since 1904
France has maintained an \"[Entente
Cordiale](Entente_Cordiale "wikilink")\" with the United Kingdom, and
there has been a strengthening of [ties between the
countries](France–United_Kingdom_relations "wikilink"), [especially
militarily](Lancaster_House_Treaties "wikilink").

France is a member of the North Atlantic Treaty Organization
([NATO](NATO "wikilink")), but under President [Charles de
Gaulle](Charles_de_Gaulle "wikilink") excluded itself from the joint
military command in protest of the [Special
Relationship](Special_Relationship "wikilink") between the U.S. and UK,
and to preserve the independence of French foreign and security
policies. Under [Nicolas Sarkozy](Nicolas_Sarkozy "wikilink"), France
rejoined the NATO joint military command.[^162][^163][^164]

France retains strong political and economic influence in its former
African colonies (_[Françafrique](Françafrique "wikilink")_)[^165] and
has supplied economic aid and troops for peacekeeping missions in [Ivory
Coast](Ivory_Coast "wikilink") and [Chad](Chad "wikilink").[^166] From
2012 to 2021, France and other African states intervened in support of
the Malian government in the [Mali War](Mali_War "wikilink").

In 2017, France was the world\'s fourth-largest donor of [development
aid](development_aid "wikilink") in absolute terms.[^167] This
represents 0.43% of its [GNP](GNP "wikilink").[^168] Aid is provided by
the governmental [French Development
Agency](French_Development_Agency "wikilink"), which finances primarily
humanitarian projects in [sub-Saharan
Africa](sub-Saharan_Africa "wikilink"),[^169] with an emphasis on
\"developing infrastructure, access to health care and education, the
implementation of appropriate economic policies and the consolidation of
the rule of law and democracy\".[^170]

### Military

The French Armed Forces
(`{{Lang|fr|Forces armées françaises}}`{=mediawiki}) are the military
and paramilitary forces of France, under the president as
commander-in-chief. They consist of the [French
Army](French_Army "wikilink")
(`{{Lang|fr|Armée de Terre}}`{=mediawiki}), the [French
Navy](French_Navy "wikilink") (_Marine Nationale_, formerly called
_Armée de Mer_), the [French Air and Space
Force](French_Air_and_Space_Force "wikilink") (_Armée de l\'Air et de
l\'Espace_), and the [National
Gendarmerie](National_Gendarmerie "wikilink") (_Gendarmerie nationale_),
which serves as both [military police](military_police "wikilink") and
civil police in rural areas. Together they are among the [largest armed
forces in the
world](List_of_countries_by_number_of_military_and_paramilitary_personnel "wikilink")
and the largest in the EU. According to a 2015 study by [Crédit
Suisse](Crédit_Suisse "wikilink"), the French Armed Forces ranked as the
world\'s sixth-most powerful military and the second most powerful in
Europe.[^171] France\'s annual military expenditure in 2023 was US\$61.3
billion, or 2.1% [of its
GDP](List_of_countries_with_highest_military_expenditures "wikilink"),
making it the eighth biggest military spender in the world.[^172] There
has been no national conscription since 1997.[^173]
![`{{Legend|#0000FF|France}}`{=mediawiki}
`{{Legend|#F0002B|French military interventions since 2001: [[War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)|Afghanistan]]; [[2004 French–Ivorian clashes|Ivory Coast]]; [[Chadian Civil War (2005–10)|Chad]]; [[2011 military intervention in Libya|Libya]]; [[Operation Enduring Freedom – Horn of Africa|Somalia]]; [[Northern Mali conflict|Mali]]; [[Central African Republic conflict under the Djotodia administration|Central African Republic]]; [[Syrian Civil War|Syria]]; [[American-led intervention in Iraq (2014–present)|Iraq]].}}`{=mediawiki}](Opérations_extérieures_depuis_2001.png " ")
France has been a [recognised nuclear
state](List_of_states_with_nuclear_weapons#Five_nuclear-weapon_states_under_the_NPT "wikilink")
since 1960. It is a party to both the [Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban
Treaty](Comprehensive_Nuclear-Test-Ban_Treaty "wikilink")[^174] and the
[Nuclear Non-Proliferation
Treaty](Treaty_on_the_Non-Proliferation_of_Nuclear_Weapons "wikilink").
The French nuclear force (formerly known as \"_[Force de
Frappe](Force_de_dissuasion "wikilink")_\") consists of four
[_Triomphant_-class submarines](Triomphant-class_submarine "wikilink")
equipped with [submarine-launched ballistic
missiles](submarine-launched_ballistic_missile "wikilink"). France has
about 60 [ASMP](Air-sol_moyenne_portée "wikilink") medium-range
[air-to-ground missiles](Air-to-surface_missile "wikilink") with
[nuclear warheads](Nuclear_weapon "wikilink");[^175] 50 are deployed by
the Air and Space Force using the [Mirage
2000N](Dassault_Mirage_2000N/2000D "wikilink") long-range nuclear strike
aircraft, while around 10 are deployed by the French Navy\'s [_Super
Étendard Modernisé_](Dassault-Breguet_Super_Étendard "wikilink") attack
aircraft, which operate from the nuclear-powered aircraft carrier
[_Charles de
Gaulle_](French_aircraft_carrier_Charles_de_Gaulle "wikilink").

France has major military industries and one of the largest [aerospace
sectors](Aerospace_manufacturer "wikilink") in the world.[^176] The
country has produced such equipment as the [Dassault
Rafale](Dassault_Rafale "wikilink") fighter, the _Charles de Gaulle_
aircraft carrier, the [Exocet](Exocet "wikilink") missile, and the
[Leclerc tank](Leclerc_tank "wikilink"), among others. France is a major
arms seller,[^177][^178] with most of its arsenal\'s designs available
for the export market, except for nuclear-powered devices. Weapons
exported totalled 27 billion euros in 2022, up from 11.7 billion euros
the previous year. The [UAE](United_Arab_Emirates "wikilink")
contributed more than 16 billion euros arms to the French total.[^179]
Among the largest French defence companies are
[Dassault](Dassault_Group "wikilink"), [Thales](Thales_Group "wikilink")
and [Safran](Safran "wikilink").[^180]

The [Directorate General for External
Security](Directorate_General_for_External_Security "wikilink") is a
component of the Armed Forces under the authority of the [Ministry of
Defence](<Ministry_of_Armed_Forces_(France)> "wikilink"). The [General
Directorate for Internal
Security](General_Directorate_for_Internal_Security "wikilink") operates
under the authority of the [Ministry of the
Interior](<Minister_of_the_Interior_(France)> "wikilink").[^181] France\'s
cybersecurity capabilities are regularly ranked as some of the most
robust of any nation in the world.[^182][^183]

### Law

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Law of France}}
```

```{=mediawiki}
{{See also|Administrative jurisdiction in France}}
```

France uses a [civil legal](<Civil_law_(legal_system)> "wikilink") system,
wherein law arises primarily from written statutes;[^184] judges are not
to make law, but merely to interpret it (though the amount of judicial
interpretation in certain areas makes it equivalent to [case
law](case_law "wikilink") in a [common law](common_law "wikilink")
system). Basic principles of the [rule of law](rule_of_law "wikilink")
were laid in the [Napoleonic Code](Napoleonic_Code "wikilink") (which
was largely based on royal law codified under King Louis XIV). In
agreement with the principles of the Declaration of the Rights of Man
and of the Citizen, the law should only prohibit actions detrimental to
society. ![The basic principles that the French Republic must respect
are found in the 1789 [Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the
Citizen](Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen "wikilink").](Declaration_of_Human_Rights.jpg "The basic principles that the French Republic must respect are found in the 1789 Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen.")

French law is divided into two principal areas: [private
law](private_law "wikilink") and [public law](public_law "wikilink").
Private law includes, in particular, [civil
law](<civil_law_(common_law)> "wikilink") and [criminal
law](criminal_law "wikilink"). Public law includes, in particular,
[administrative law](administrative_law "wikilink") and [constitutional
law](constitutional_law "wikilink"). However, in practical terms, French
law comprises three principal areas of law: civil law, criminal law, and
administrative law. Criminal laws can only address the future and not
the past (criminal _[ex post facto](Ex_post_facto_law "wikilink")_ laws
are prohibited).[^185] While administrative law is often a subcategory
of civil law in many countries, it is completely separated in France and
each body of law is headed by a specific supreme court: [ordinary
courts](French_judiciary_courts "wikilink") (which handle criminal and
civil litigation) are headed by the [Court of
Cassation](<Court_of_Cassation_(France)> "wikilink") and administrative
courts are headed by the [Council of
State](<Conseil_d'Etat_(France)> "wikilink"). To be applicable, every law
must be officially published in the _[Journal officiel de la République
française](Journal_officiel_de_la_République_française "wikilink")_.

### Human rights {#human_rights}

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Human rights in France}}
```

France does not recognise [religious law](religious_law "wikilink") as a
motivation for the enactment of prohibitions; it has long abolished
[blasphemy](blasphemy "wikilink") laws and [sodomy
laws](sodomy_law "wikilink"). However, \"offences against public
[decency](decency "wikilink")\" or [disturbing public
order](breach_of_the_peace "wikilink") have been used to repress public
expressions of homosexuality or street
prostitution.`{{Citation needed|date=May 2024}}`{=mediawiki}

France generally has a positive reputation regarding [LGBTQ
rights](LGBTQ_rights_in_France "wikilink").[^186] Since 1999, [civil
unions](Pacte_civil_de_solidarité "wikilink") for homosexual couples
have been permitted, and since 2013, [same-sex
marriage](same-sex_marriage "wikilink") and [LGBT
adoption](LGBT_adoption "wikilink") are legal.[^187] France has laws
against racism and [antisemitism](antisemitism "wikilink"),[^188] while
the 1990 [Gayssot Act](Gayssot_Act "wikilink") prohibits [Holocaust
denial](Holocaust_denial "wikilink"). In 2024, France became the first
nation in the European Union to explicitly protect
[abortion](abortion "wikilink") in its
[constitution](Constitution_of_France "wikilink").[^189]

[Freedom of religion](Freedom_of_religion_in_France "wikilink") is
constitutionally guaranteed by the 1789 Declaration of the Rights of Man
and of the Citizen. The [1905 French law on the Separation of the
Churches and the
State](1905_French_law_on_the_Separation_of_the_Churches_and_the_State "wikilink")
is the basis for _[laïcité](laïcité "wikilink")_ (state secularism): the
state does not formally recognise any religion, [except in
Alsace-Moselle](Concordat_in_Alsace-Moselle "wikilink"), which continues
to subsidise education and clergy of Catholicism, Lutheranism,
Calvinism, and Judaism. Nonetheless, France does recognise religious
associations. The Parliament has [listed many religious movements as
dangerous
cults](Groups_referred_to_as_cults_in_government_reports#France "wikilink")
since 1995 and has [banned wearing conspicuous religious symbols in
schools](French_law_on_secularity_and_conspicuous_religious_symbols_in_schools "wikilink")
since 2004. In 2010, it banned the [wearing of face-covering Islamic
veils in public](French_ban_on_face_covering "wikilink"); human rights
groups such as [Amnesty International](Amnesty_International "wikilink")
and [Human Rights Watch](Human_Rights_Watch "wikilink") described the
law as discriminatory towards Muslims.[^190][^191] However, it is
supported by most of the population.[^192]

## Economy

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Economy of France}}
```

```{=mediawiki}
{{further|Science and technology in France}}
```

![[La Défense](La_Défense "wikilink") was in 2017 ranked by [Ernst &
Young](Ernst_&_Young "wikilink") as the leading [central business
district](central_business_district "wikilink") in continental Europe,
and fourth in the
world.[^193]](04-2017._París-34.jpg "La Défense was in 2017 ranked by Ernst & Young as the leading central business district in continental Europe, and fourth in the world.")
France
has a [social market economy](social_market_economy "wikilink")
characterised by sizeable government involvement and [diversified
sectors](economic_diversity "wikilink"). For two centuries, it has
[consistently
ranked](List_of_countries_by_largest_historical_GDP "wikilink") among
