the ten largest globally; `{{as of|2025|lc=yes}}`{=mediawiki} it is the
[world\'s ninth largest](<List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)> "wikilink") by
[purchasing power parity](purchasing_power_parity "wikilink") and second
largest in the EU, after Germany.[^195] Considered a [great
power](great_power "wikilink") with considerable [economic
strength](Economic_power "wikilink"),[^196] it is a member of the [Group
of Seven](Group_of_Seven "wikilink") leading industrialised countries,
the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and
Development](OECD "wikilink") (OECD), and the [G20](G20 "wikilink").
France ranked 13th in the 2025 [Global Innovation
Index](Global_Innovation_Index "wikilink").[^197][^198]

The economy is highly diversified; services represent two-thirds of both
the workforce and GDP,[^199] while the industrial sector accounts for a
fifth of GDP and a similar proportion of employment. France is the
third-biggest manufacturing country in Europe, behind Germany and Italy,
and ranks [eighth in the world by manufacturing
output](List_of_countries_by_manufacturing_output "wikilink"), at 1.9
per cent.[^200] Less than 2 per cent of GDP is generated by the primary
sector, namely agriculture;[^201] the agricultural sector is among the
largest in value and leads the EU in overall production.[^202]

In 2018 France was the [fifth-largest trading
nation](List_of_countries_by_total_trade "wikilink") and second-largest
in Europe, with the value of exports representing over a fifth of
GDP.[^203] Its membership in the [eurozone](eurozone "wikilink") and the
broader [European single market](European_single_market "wikilink")
facilitates access to capital, goods, services, and skilled
labour.[^204] Despite [protectionist](Protectionism "wikilink") policies
over certain industries, particularly in agriculture, France has
generally played a leading role in fostering free trade and commercial
integration in Europe to enhance its economy.[^205][^206] In 2019, it
ranked first in Europe and [13th in the
world](List_of_countries_by_foreign_direct_investment_inflows "wikilink")
in [foreign direct investment](foreign_direct_investment "wikilink"),
with European countries and the United States being leading
sources.[^207] According to the [Bank of
France](Bank_of_France "wikilink") (founded in 1800),[^208] the leading
recipients of foreign direct investment are manufacturing, real estate,
finance and insurance.[^209] The [Paris
Region](Île-de-France "wikilink") has the highest concentration of
multinational firms in mainland Europe.[^210]

Under the doctrine of _[dirigisme](dirigisme "wikilink")_, the
government historically played a major role in the economy; policies
such as [indicative planning](indicative_planning "wikilink") and
[nationalisation](Nationalization "wikilink") are credited for
contributing to three decades of unprecedented postwar economic growth
known as _[Trente Glorieuses](Trente_Glorieuses "wikilink")_. At its
peak in 1982, the public sector accounted for one-fifth of industrial
employment and over four-fifths of the credit market. Beginning in the
late 20th century, regulations and state involvement in the economy were
loosened, with most leading companies becoming privately owned; state
ownership still dominates transportation, defence and
broadcasting.[^211] Policies aimed at promoting economic dynamism and
privatisation have improved France\'s economic standing globally: it is
among the world\'s 10 most innovative countries in the 2020 [Bloomberg
Innovation Index](Bloomberg_L.P. "wikilink"),[^212] and the 15th most
competitive according to the 2019 [Global Competitiveness
Report](Global_Competitiveness_Report#2019_rankings "wikilink").[^213]

The [Paris stock exchange](Euronext_Paris "wikilink") is one of the
oldest in the world, created in 1724.[^214] Some examples of the most
valuable French companies include [LVMH](LVMH "wikilink"),
[L\'Oréal](L'Oréal "wikilink") and [Sociéte
Générale](Société_Générale "wikilink").[^215] France has historically
been one of the world\'s major agricultural centres and remains a
\"global agricultural powerhouse\"; France is the world\'s sixth-biggest
exporter of agricultural products, generating a trade surplus of over
€7.4 billion.[^216][^217] Nicknamed \"the granary of the old
continent\",[^218] over half its total land area is farmland, of which
45 per cent is devoted to permanent field crops such as cereals. The
country\'s diverse climate, extensive arable land, modern farming
technology, and [EU subsidies](Common_Agricultural_Policy "wikilink")
have made it Europe\'s leading agricultural producer and exporter.[^219]

### Tourism

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Tourism in France}}
```

![[Palace of Versailles](Palace_of_Versailles "wikilink"),
[Yvelines](Yvelines "wikilink")](Vue_aérienne_du_domaine_de_Versailles_par_ToucanWings_-_Creative_Commons_By_Sa_3.0_-_081.jpg "Palace of Versailles, Yvelines")
With 102 million international tourist arrivals in 2025, France is the
[world\'s top tourist
destination](World_Tourism_rankings "wikilink")[^220] and ranks third in
tourism-derived income due to shorter visit durations.[^221] The most
popular tourist sites include (annual visitors): [Eiffel
Tower](Eiffel_Tower "wikilink") (6.2 million), [Château de
Versailles](Palace_of_Versailles "wikilink") (2.8 million),
`{{Lang|fr|[[National Museum of Natural History (France)|Muséum national d'Histoire naturelle]]|italic=no}}`{=mediawiki}
(2 million), [Pont du Gard](Pont_du_Gard "wikilink") (1.5 million), [Arc
de Triomphe](Arc_de_Triomphe "wikilink") (1.2 million), [Mont
Saint-Michel](Mont_Saint-Michel "wikilink") (1 million),
[Sainte-Chapelle](Sainte-Chapelle "wikilink") (683,000), [Château du
Haut-Kœnigsbourg](Château_du_Haut-Kœnigsbourg "wikilink") (549,000),
[Puy de Dôme](Puy_de_Dôme "wikilink") (500,000), [Musée
Picasso](Musée_Picasso "wikilink") (441,000), and
[Carcassonne](Carcassonne "wikilink") (362,000).[^222]

France, especially Paris, has some of the world\'s largest museums
including the [Louvre](Louvre "wikilink"), which is the [most visited
art museum in the world](List_of_most-visited_art_museums "wikilink")
(7.7 million visitors in 2022); the [Musée
d\'Orsay](Musée_d'Orsay "wikilink") (3.3 million), mostly devoted to
[Impressionism](Impressionism "wikilink") and voted best museum in the
world in 2018;[^223] the [Musée de
l\'Orangerie](Musée_de_l'Orangerie "wikilink") (1.02 million), which is
home to eight large [Water Lily](<Water_Lilies_(Monet_series)> "wikilink")
murals by [Claude Monet](Claude_Monet "wikilink"); and the [Centre
Pompidou](Centre_Pompidou "wikilink") (3 million), dedicated to
[contemporary art](contemporary_art "wikilink").

[Disneyland Paris](Disneyland_Paris "wikilink") is Europe\'s most
popular theme park, with 15 million combined visitors to the resort\'s
[Disneyland Park](<Disneyland_Park_(Paris)> "wikilink") and [Walt Disney
Studios Park](Walt_Disney_Studios_Park "wikilink") in 2009.[^224] With
more than 10 million tourists per year, the [French
Riviera](French_Riviera "wikilink") is the second leading tourist
destination in the country, after the Paris Region.[^225] With 6 million
tourists per year, the [castles of the Loire
Valley](Châteaux_of_the_Loire_Valley "wikilink") (French: _châteaux_)
and the [Loire Valley](Loire_Valley "wikilink") are the third leading
tourist destination.[^226][^227]

France has [54 sites](List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_France "wikilink")
inscribed on the [UNESCO World Heritage
List](World_Heritage_Site#Statistics "wikilink"), ranking it fourth
worldwide by number of sites.[^228][^229] France features cities of high
cultural interest, beaches and seaside resorts, ski resorts, as well as
rural regions that many enjoy for their beauty and tranquillity ([green
tourism](Ecotourism "wikilink")). Small and picturesque villages are
promoted through the association
`{{Lang|fr|[[Les Plus Beaux Villages de France]]}}`{=mediawiki}
(literally \"The Most Beautiful Villages of France\"). The \"[Remarkable
Gardens](List_of_Remarkable_Gardens_of_France "wikilink")\" label is a
list of the over 200 gardens classified by the [Ministry of
Culture](<Ministry_of_Culture_(France)> "wikilink"). This label is
intended to protect and promote remarkable gardens and parks. France
attracts many religious [pilgrims](Christian_pilgrimage "wikilink") on
their [way to St. James](Camino_de_Santiago "wikilink") or to
[Lourdes](Lourdes "wikilink"), a town in the
[Hautes-Pyrénées](Hautes-Pyrénées "wikilink") that hosts several million
visitors a year.

### Energy

```{=mediawiki}
{{Further|Energy in France}}
```

![[Belleville Nuclear Power
Plant](Belleville_Nuclear_Power_Plant "wikilink"). France derives most
of its electricity from [nuclear
power](Nuclear_power_in_France "wikilink"), the [highest percentage in
the
world](Nuclear_power_by_country "wikilink").](Centrale_Nucléaire-Belleville-sur-Loire-A02.JPG "Belleville Nuclear Power Plant. France derives most of its electricity from nuclear power, the highest percentage in the world.")
France is the world\'s tenth-largest producer of electricity.[^230]
[Électricité de France](Électricité_de_France "wikilink") (EDF), which
is majority-owned by the French government, is the country\'s main
producer and distributor of electricity and one of the world\'s largest
[electric utility](electric_utility "wikilink") companies, ranking third
in revenue globally.[^231] In 2018, EDF produced roughly one-fifth of
the EU\'s electricity, primarily from [nuclear
power](nuclear_power "wikilink").[^232] In 2021, France was the biggest
energy exporter in Europe, mostly to the UK and Italy,[^233] and the
largest net exporter of electricity in the world.[^234]

Since the [1973 oil crisis](1973_oil_crisis "wikilink"), France has
pursued a strong policy of [energy
security](energy_security "wikilink"),[^235] namely through [heavy
investment in nuclear energy](Nuclear_power_in_France "wikilink"). It is
one of 32 countries with [nuclear power
plants](nuclear_power_plant "wikilink"), ranking second in the world by
the [number of operational nuclear
reactors](Nuclear_power_by_country "wikilink") at 56.[^236]
Consequently, 70% of its electricity is generated by nuclear power, the
highest proportion in the world by a wide margin;[^237] only Slovakia
and Ukraine also derive a majority of electricity from nuclear power, at
roughly 53% and 51%, respectively.[^238] France is considered a world
leader in nuclear technology, with reactors and fuel products being
major exports.[^239]

The significant reliance on nuclear power has resulted in comparatively
slower [adoption of renewable
energy](Renewable_energy_in_France "wikilink") relative to other Western
nations. Nevertheless, between 2008 and 2019, its production capacity
from renewable energies rose consistently and nearly doubled.[^240]
[Hydropower](Hydropower "wikilink") is by far the leading source,
accounting for over half of renewable energy sources[^241] and
contributing 13% of its electricity[^242] the third highest proportion
in Europe.[^243] Most hydroelectric plants, such as
[Eguzon](Eguzon_dam "wikilink"), [Étang de
Soulcem](Étang_de_Soulcem "wikilink"), and [Lac de
Vouglans](Lac_de_Vouglans "wikilink"), are managed by EDF.[^244] France
aims to further expand hydropower into 2040.[^245]

### Transport

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Transport in France}}
```

![[Millau Viaduct](Millau_Viaduct "wikilink") is the [tallest bridge in
the
world](List_of_tallest_bridges "wikilink").[^246]](Creissels_et_Viaduct_de_Millau.jpg "Millau Viaduct is the tallest bridge in the world.")
The [railway network in France](Rail_transport_in_France "wikilink")
stretches `{{Convert|29473|km|mi|0}}`{=mediawiki} as of 2008[^248] and
is the second most extensive in Western Europe after
[Germany\'s](Rail_transport_in_Germany "wikilink").[^249] It is operated
by the [SNCF](SNCF "wikilink"), and high-speed trains include
[Thalys](Thalys "wikilink"), [Eurostar](Eurostar "wikilink") and
[TGV](TGV "wikilink"), which travels at
`{{Convert|320|km/h|mph|0|abbr=on}}`{=mediawiki}.[^250] Eurostar and
[LeShuttle](LeShuttle "wikilink") connect with the United Kingdom
through the [Channel Tunnel](Channel_Tunnel "wikilink"). Rail
connections exist to all other neighbouring countries in Europe except
Andorra. Intra-urban connections are also well developed, with most
major cities having [underground](Rapid_transit "wikilink") or tramway
services complementing bus services.

There are approximately `{{Convert|1027183|km|mi|0}}`{=mediawiki} of
serviceable roadway, ranking it the most extensive network of the
European continent.[^251] The Paris Region is enveloped with a dense
network of roads and highways, which connect it with virtually all parts
of the country. Roads also handle substantial international traffic,
connecting with cities in neighbouring countries. There is no annual
registration fee or [road tax](road_tax "wikilink"); usage of the mostly
privately owned motorways is through tolls except in the vicinity of
large communes. The new car market is dominated by domestic brands such
as [Renault](Renault "wikilink"), [Peugeot](Peugeot "wikilink") and
[Citroën](Citroën "wikilink").[^252] France possesses the [Millau
Viaduct](Millau_Viaduct "wikilink"), the world\'s tallest bridge,[^253]
and has built many important bridges such as the [Pont de
Normandie](Pont_de_Normandie "wikilink"). Diesel and petrol-driven cars
and lorries cause a large part of the country\'s air pollution and
greenhouse gas emissions.[^254][^255]

There are 464 [airports in
France](List_of_airports_in_France "wikilink").[^256] [Charles de Gaulle
Airport](Charles_de_Gaulle_Airport "wikilink"), located in the vicinity
of Paris, is the largest and busiest airport in the country, handling
the vast majority of popular and commercial traffic and connecting Paris
with virtually all major cities across the world. [Air
France](Air_France "wikilink") is the national carrier airline, although
numerous private airline companies provide domestic and international
travel services. There are ten major ports, the largest of which is in
[Marseille](Marseille "wikilink"),[^257] which also is the largest
bordering the Mediterranean Sea.[^258]
`{{Convert|12261|km|mi|0}}`{=mediawiki} of waterways traverse France
including the [Canal du Midi](Canal_du_Midi "wikilink"), which connects
the Mediterranean Sea to the Atlantic Ocean through the
[Garonne](Garonne "wikilink") river.[^259]

## Demographics

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Demographics of France}}
```

[upright=1.2\|thumb\|Population density in France by
[arrondissement](Arrondissements_of_France "wikilink")](File:Population_density_in_France.png "wikilink")

With an estimated population of 69,081,996
people,[^260]`{{efn|As of January 2026}}`{=mediawiki} France is the
[20th most populous country in the
world](List_of_countries_by_population#Sovereign_states_and_dependencies_by_population "wikilink"),
the third-most populous in Europe, and the second most populous in the
European Union. For much of the 21st century, France has been an outlier
among developed countries, particularly in Europe, for its relatively
high rate of [natural population
growth](Rate_of_natural_increase "wikilink"); by birth rates alone, it
was responsible for almost all natural population growth in the EU in 2006.[^261] Between 2006 and 2016, France saw the second-highest overall
increase in population in the EU and was one of four EU countries where
natural births accounted for the most population growth.[^262] This was
the highest rate since the end of the [baby boom](baby_boom "wikilink")
in 1973 and coincides with the rise in the [total fertility
rate](total_fertility_rate "wikilink") from a nadir of 1.7 in 1994 to
2.0 in 2010.

Since 2011, the fertility rate has been steadily declining;[^263] it
stood at 1.79 per woman in 2023,[^264] below the [replacement
rate](Total_fertility_rate "wikilink") of 2.1 and well below the high of
4.41 in 1800.[^265][^266][^267] Fertility rate and crude [birth
rate](birth_rate "wikilink") nonetheless remain the highest in the
EU[^268] and among the highest in Europe overall, where the average is
1.5.[^269] The mean age of French women at the birth of their first
child was 29.1, slightly younger than the EU average of 29.7.[^270]

Like many developed nations, the [population is
aging](Population_ageing "wikilink"): The average age is 41.7 years,
while roughly one-fifth of French people are 65 or over.[^271] It is
projected that one in three French will be over 60 by 2024. Life
expectancy at birth is 82.7 years, the [12th highest in the
world](List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy "wikilink"); French
Polynesia and Réunion ranked fourth and 11th in life expectancy, at
84.07 years and 83.55, respectively.

From 2006 to 2011, population growth averaged 0.6 percent per
year;[^272] since 2011, annual growth has been between 0.4 and 0.5
percent annually,[^273] and France is projected to continue growing
until 2044.[^274] Immigrants are major contributors to this trend; in
2010, roughly one in four newborns (27 percent) in Metropolitan France
had at least one
[foreign-born](Immigration_to_France#Immigration_per_region "wikilink")
parent, and another 24 percent had at least one parent born outside
Europe (excluding French overseas territories).[^275] In 2021, the share
of children of foreign-born mothers was 23 percent.[^276]

The fertility rate in France dropped to 1.56 in 2025. This was the
lowest rate since the end of the first world war.[^277]

### Major cities {#major_cities}

```{=mediawiki}
{{See also|Functional area (France)}}
```

France is a highly urbanised country, with its [largest
cities](<List_of_cities_in_France_over_20,000_population_(1999_census)> "wikilink")
(in terms of [metropolitan area](<Functional_area_(France)> "wikilink")
population in 2021[^278]) being Paris (13,171,056 inh.),
[Lyon](Lyon "wikilink") (2,308,818), [Marseille](Marseille "wikilink")
(1,888,788), [Lille](Lille "wikilink") (1,521,660),
[Toulouse](Toulouse "wikilink") (1,490,640),
[Bordeaux](Bordeaux "wikilink") (1,393,764), [Nantes](Nantes "wikilink")
(1,031,953), [Strasbourg](Strasbourg "wikilink") (864,993),
[Montpellier](Montpellier "wikilink") (823,120), and
[Rennes](Rennes "wikilink") (771,320). (Note: since its 2020 revision of
metropolitan area borders, [INSEE](INSEE "wikilink") considers that
[Nice](Nice "wikilink") is a metropolitan area separate from the
[Cannes](Cannes "wikilink")-[Antibes](Antibes "wikilink") metropolitan
area; these two combined would have a population of 1,019,905, as of the
2021 census). [Rural flight](Rural_flight "wikilink") was a perennial
political issue throughout most of the 20th century.
`{{Largest metropolitan areas of France}}`{=mediawiki}

### Ethnic groups {#ethnic_groups}

Historically, [French people](French_people "wikilink") were mainly of
[Celtic](Celtic_peoples "wikilink")-[Gallic](Gauls "wikilink") origin,
with a significant admixture of [Italic](Italic_peoples "wikilink")
([Romans](Roman_Empire "wikilink")) and
[Germanic](Germanic_peoples "wikilink") ([Franks](Franks "wikilink"))
groups reflecting centuries of respective migration and
settlement.[^279] Through the course of the Middle Ages, France
incorporated various neighbouring ethnic and linguistic groups, as
evidenced by [Breton](Bretons "wikilink") elements in the west,
[Aquitanian](Aquitani "wikilink") in the southwest,
[Scandinavian](Scandinavia "wikilink") in the northwest,
[Alemannic](Alemanni "wikilink") in the northeast, and
[Ligurian](Ligures "wikilink") in the southeast.

Large-scale immigration over the last century and a half have led to a
more multicultural society; beginning with the French Revolution and
further codified in the French Constitution of 1958, the government is
prohibited from collecting data on ethnicity and ancestry; most
demographic information is drawn from private sector organisations or
academic institutions. In 2004 the _Institut Montaigne_ estimated that
within Metropolitan France, 51 million people were white (85% of the
population), 6 million were northwest African (10%), 2 million were
black (3.3%), and 1 million were Asian (1.7%).[^280][^281]

A 2008 poll conducted jointly by the
`{{lang|fr|[[Institut national d'études démographiques]]|italic=no}}`{=mediawiki}
(INSEE) and [the French National Institute of
Statistics](Institut_national_de_la_statistique_et_des_études_économiques "wikilink")[^282][^283]
estimated that the largest minority ancestry groups were
[Italian](Italians_in_France "wikilink") (5 million), followed by
[northwest African](Maghreb "wikilink") (3--6
million),[^284][^285][^286] [Sub-Saharan
African](Black_French_people "wikilink") (2.5 million), Armenian
(500,000), and Turkish (200,000).[^287] There are also sizeable
minorities of other [European ethnic
groups](Ethnic_groups_in_Europe "wikilink"), namely
[Spanish](Spaniards "wikilink"),
[Portuguese](Portuguese_people "wikilink"),
[Polish](Polish_people "wikilink"), and
[Greek](Greeks "wikilink").[^288][^289][^290] France has a significant
[Gitan (Romani)](Romani_people_in_France "wikilink") population,
numbering between 20,000 and 400,000;[^291] many foreign
[Roma](Romani_people "wikilink") are [expelled back to Bulgaria and
Romania
frequently](Deportation_of_Roma_migrants_from_France "wikilink").[^292]

### Immigration

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Immigration to France}}
```

It is estimated that 40% of the French population is descended at least
partially from the different waves of immigration since the early 20th
century;[^293] between 1921 and 1935 alone, about 1.1 million net
immigrants came to France.[^294] The next largest wave came in the 1960s
when around 1.6 million _[pieds-noirs](pieds-noirs "wikilink")_ returned
to France following the independence of its northwest African
possessions of Algeria and Morocco.[^295][^296] They were joined by
numerous former colonial subjects from North and West Africa, as well as
numerous European immigrants from Spain and Portugal.

![The [Calais Jungle](Calais_Jungle "wikilink") was a
[refugee](refugee "wikilink") and [illegal
migrant](Economic_migrant "wikilink") encampment in the vicinity of
[Calais](Calais "wikilink"), France, that existed from January 2015 to
October
2016.](Calais2015a.jpg "The Calais Jungle was a refugee and illegal migrant encampment in the vicinity of Calais, France, that existed from January 2015 to October 2016.")
France remains a major destination for immigrants, accepting about
200,000 legal immigrants annually.[^297] In 2005, it was Western
Europe\'s leading recipient of asylum seekers, with an estimated 50,000
applications (albeit a 15% decrease from 2004).[^298] In 2010, France
received about 48,100 asylum applications---placing it among the top
five asylum recipients in the world.[^299] In subsequent years it saw
the number of applications increase, ultimately doubling to 100,412 in 2017.[^300] The European Union allows free movement between the member
states, although France established controls to curb Eastern European
migration. Foreigners\' rights are established in the [Code of Entry and
Residence of Foreigners and of the Right to
Asylum](Code_of_Entry_and_Residence_of_Foreigners_and_of_the_Right_to_Asylum "wikilink").
Immigration remains a contentious political issue.[^301]

In 2008 INSEE estimated that the number of foreign-born immigrants was
around 5 million (8% of the population), while their French-born
descendants numbered 6.5 million, or 11% of the population. Thus, nearly
a fifth of the country\'s population were either first or
second-generation immigrants, of which more than 5 million were of
European origin and 4 million of [Maghrebi](Maghrebis "wikilink")
ancestry.[^302][^303][^304] In 2008, France [granted
citizenship](French_nationality_law "wikilink") to 137,000 persons,
mostly from Morocco, Algeria and Turkey.[^305] In 2022, more than
320,000 migrants came to France, with the majority coming from
[Africa](Africa "wikilink").[^306]

In 2014, the INSEE reported a significant increase in the number of
immigrants coming from Spain, Portugal and Italy between 2009 and 2012.
According to the institute, this increase resulted from the [2008
financial crisis](2008_financial_crisis "wikilink").[^307] Statistics on
Spanish immigrants in France show a growth of 107 per cent between 2009
and 2012, with the population growing from 5,300 to 11,000.[^308] Of the
total of 229,000 foreigners coming to France in 2012, nearly 8% were
Portuguese, 5% British, 5% Spanish, 4% Italian, 4% German, 3% Romanian,
and 3% Belgian.[^309]

### Language

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|French language|Languages of France|Organisation internationale de la Francophonie}}
```

[thumb\|upright=1.5\|alt=world map of French speaking countries\|Map of
the
[Francophone](List_of_territorial_entities_where_French_is_an_official_language "wikilink")
world: `{{Legend|#0c5eb1|Native language}}`{=mediawiki}
`{{Legend|#0080ff|Administrative language}}`{=mediawiki}
`{{Legend|#9fceff|Secondary or cultural language}}`{=mediawiki}
](File:Map-Francophone_World.svg "wikilink") The official language is
French,[^310] a [Romance language](Romance_language "wikilink") derived
from [Latin](Latin "wikilink"). Since 1635, the [Académie
Française](Académie_Française "wikilink") has been the official
authority on the French language, although its recommendations carry no
legal weight. There are also regional languages spoken in France, such
as [Occitan](Occitan_language "wikilink"),
[Breton](Breton_language "wikilink"),
[Catalan](Catalan_language "wikilink"),
[Flemish](French_Flemish "wikilink") ([Dutch](Dutch_language "wikilink")
dialect), [Alsatian](Alsatian_dialect "wikilink") (German dialect),
[Basque](Basque_language "wikilink"), and
[Corsican](Corsican_language "wikilink") (Italian dialect). Italian was
the official language of Corsica up to 1859.[^311] Although regional
languages do not have the status of official languages, they are
recognised by Article 75-1 of the constitution as part of France\'s
heritage.[^312]

The government does not regulate the choice of language in publications
by individuals, but the use of French is required by law in commercial
and workplace communications. The French government tries to promote
French in the EU and globally through institutions such as the
`{{Lang|fr|[[Organisation internationale de la Francophonie]]|italic=no}}`{=mediawiki}.
There are 77 vernacular minority languages, eight spoken in Metropolitan
France and 69 in the overseas territories.

According to the 2007 Adult Education Survey, part of a project by the
European Union and carried out in France by INSEE and based on a sample
of 15,350 persons, French was the native language of 87% of the
population, or roughly 56 million people, followed by Arabic (3.6%,
2.3 million), Portuguese (1.5%, 960,000), Spanish (1.2%, 770,000) and
Italian (1.0%, 640,000).[^313]

### Religion

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Religion in France}}
```

![[Notre-Dame de Reims](Reims_Cathedral "wikilink") is the Catholic
cathedral where the [Kings of France were
crowned](Coronation_of_the_French_monarch "wikilink") until
1825.
`{{efn|The last ''sacre'' was that of [[Charles X of France|Charles X]] on 29 May 1825.}}`{=mediawiki}](<Reims_Cathédrale_Notre-Dame_5002_(fixed_angles).jpg> "Notre-Dame de Reims is the Catholic cathedral where the Kings of France were crowned until 1825.")
France is a secular country in which [freedom of
religion](freedom_of_religion "wikilink") is a constitutional right.
After alternating between royal and secular republican governments
during the 19th century, the [1905 law on the Separation of the Churches
and the
State](1905_French_law_on_the_Separation_of_the_Churches_and_the_State "wikilink")
established the concept of _[laïcité](Secularism_in_France "wikilink")_,
a strict [separation of church and
state](separation_of_church_and_state "wikilink") under which the
government and public life are kept completely secular, detached from
any religion.[^314] [Alsace](Alsace "wikilink") and
[Moselle](<Moselle_(department)> "wikilink"), which were part of the
[German Empire](German_Empire "wikilink") when state secularism was
established in France, is an exception since the [local
law](Concordat_in_Alsace–Moselle "wikilink") stipulates official status
and state funding for [Lutheranism](Lutheranism "wikilink"),
[Catholicism](Catholicism "wikilink"), and
[Judaism](Judaism "wikilink").[^315]

[Catholicism](Catholic_Church_in_France "wikilink") has been the main
religion in France for more than a millennium, and it was once the
[state religion](state_religion "wikilink").[^316] Its role nowadays has
been greatly reduced; nevertheless in 2012, among the 47,000 religious
buildings in France, 94% were Catholic churches.[^317] The government is
prohibited from recognising specific rights to any religious community
(with the exception of legacy statutes like those of military chaplains
and the aforementioned local law in Alsace-Moselle). It recognises
religious organisations according to formal legal criteria that do not
address religious doctrine, and religious organisations are expected to
refrain from intervening in policymaking.[^318] Some religious groups,
such as [scientology](scientology "wikilink"), the [Children of
God](The_Family_International "wikilink"), the [Unification
Church](Unification_Church "wikilink"), and the [Order of the Solar
Temple](Order_of_the_Solar_Temple "wikilink"), are considered
[cults](cult "wikilink") (_sectes_ in French) and are not granted the
same status as recognised religions.[^319]

### Health

![The [Pitié-Salpêtrière
Hospital](Pitié-Salpêtrière_Hospital "wikilink"), a teaching hospital in
Paris, is one of Europe's largest
hospitals.[^320]](P1000513_Paris_XIII_Salpetrière_reductwk.JPG "The Pitié-Salpêtrière Hospital, a teaching hospital in Paris, is one of Europe's largest hospitals.")
The [French health care system](Health_care_in_France "wikilink") is one
of [universal health care](universal_health_care "wikilink") largely
financed by government [national health
insurance](national_health_insurance "wikilink"). In its 2000 assessment
of world health care systems, the [World Health
Organization](World_Health_Organization "wikilink") (WHO) found that
France provided the \"close to best overall health care\" in the
world.[^322] The French health care system was ranked first worldwide by
the WHO in 1997.[^323][^324] In 2011, France spent 11.6% of its GDP on
health care, or US\$4,086 per capita,[^325] a figure much higher than
the average spent by countries in Europe. Approximately 77% of health
expenditures are covered by government-funded agencies.[^326]

Care is generally free for people affected by chronic diseases such as
cancer, AIDS or cystic fibrosis. The [life
expectancy](life_expectancy "wikilink") at birth is 80 years for men and
85.6 years for women.[^327][^328] There are 3.2 physicians for every
1000 inhabitants, 5.7 hospital beds for every 1000 inhabitants, and
average health care spending per capita was US\$8,630 in 2022,
representing 12.1% of GDP.[^329][^330] `{{As of|2007}}`{=mediawiki},
approximately 140,000 inhabitants (0.4%) are living with HIV/AIDS.[^331]

### Education

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Education in France}}
```

![The [ENS](École_normale_supérieure_(Paris) "wikilink") produces among
the most [Nobel Prize](Nobel_Prize "wikilink") laureates [per
capita](per_capita "wikilink") in the
world.[^332]](Façade_de_l'École_normale_supérieure.JPG "The ENS produces among the most Nobel Prize laureates per capita in the world.")
In 1802, Napoleon created the
[lycée](Secondary_education_in_France "wikilink"), the second and final
stage of secondary education that prepares students for higher education
studies or a profession.[^334] [Jules Ferry](Jules_Ferry "wikilink") is
considered the father of the French modern school, leading reforms in
the late 19th century that established free, secular and compulsory
education (currently mandatory to age 16).[^335][^336]

French education is centralised and divided into three stages: primary,
secondary, and higher education. The [Programme for International
Student
Assessment](Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment "wikilink"),
coordinated by the OECD, ranked France\'s education as near the OECD
average in 2018.[^337][^338] School children in France reported greater
concern about the disciplinary climate and behaviour in classrooms
compared to other OECD countries.[^339]

Higher education is divided between [public
universities](List_of_universities_and_colleges_in_France "wikilink")
and the prestigious and selective *[Grandes
écoles](Grande*école "wikilink")*, such as [Sciences Po
Paris](Sciences_Po "wikilink") for political studies, [HEC
Paris](HEC_Paris "wikilink") for economics,
[Polytechnique](École_Polytechnique "wikilink"), the [École des hautes
études en sciences
sociales](École_des_hautes*études*en_sciences_sociales "wikilink") for
social studies and the [École nationale supérieure des mines de
Paris](École_nationale_supérieure_des_mines_de_Paris "wikilink") that
produce high-profile engineers, or the [École nationale
d\'administration](École_nationale_d'administration "wikilink") for
careers in the [Grands Corps](Grands_corps_de_l'État "wikilink") of the
state. The \_Grandes écoles* have been criticised for alleged elitism,
producing many if not most of France\'s high-ranking civil servants,
CEOs and politicians.[^340]

France also supports a network of schools internationally through the
[Agency for French Education
Abroad](Agency_for_French_Education_Abroad "wikilink"). The network
includes a mix of directly managed schools, subsidized institutions, and
partner schools, and provides French education to students around the
world.[^341][^342]

## Culture

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Culture of France}}
```

### Art

```{=mediawiki}
{{Further|French art|Art in Paris}}
```

[thumb\|upright=.7\|[Claude Monet](Claude_Monet "wikilink"), founder of
the [Impressionist](Impressionism "wikilink")
movement](File:Claude_Monet_1899_Nadar_crop.jpg "wikilink")

The origins of a distinctly [French art](French_art "wikilink") date
back to the Middle Ages. In the 12th century, Paris and its region saw
the birth of [Gothic art](Gothic_art "wikilink"), known at the time as
_Opus Francigenum_ (`{{lit|French work}}`{=mediawiki}), which from the
cathedrals of [Chartres](Chartres_Cathedral "wikilink"),
[Amiens](Amiens_Cathedral "wikilink") and
[Reims](Reims_Cathedral "wikilink") soon radiated throughout Europe,
thanks to French architects and sculptors who went abroad.[^343]

French art was very much influenced first by [Flemish
art](Flemish_painting "wikilink") then by [Italian
art](Italian_art "wikilink") at the time of the
[Renaissance](Renaissance "wikilink"). [Jean
Fouquet](Jean_Fouquet "wikilink"), the most famous medieval [French
painter](List_of_French_painters "wikilink"), is said to have been the
first to travel to Italy and experience the Early Renaissance firsthand.
The Renaissance painting [School of
Fontainebleau](School_of_Fontainebleau "wikilink") was directly inspired
by Italian painters such as [Primaticcio](Primaticcio "wikilink") and
[Rosso Fiorentino](Rosso_Fiorentino "wikilink"), who both worked in
France. Two of the most famous French artists of the time of the
[Baroque](Baroque "wikilink") era, [Nicolas
Poussin](Nicolas_Poussin "wikilink") and [Claude
Lorrain](Claude_Lorrain "wikilink"), lived in Italy. But [17th-century
French art](17th-century_French_art "wikilink") quickly moved towards
classicism under [Louis XIV](Louis_XIV "wikilink"), with artists like
painter [Charles Le Brun](Charles_Le_Brun "wikilink"), architect [Louis
Le Vau](Louis_Le_Vau "wikilink"), garden and landscape architect [André
Le Nôtre](André_Le_Nôtre "wikilink") and cabinetmaker [André-Charles
Boulle](André-Charles_Boulle "wikilink") who all worked at the
construction of the [Palace of
Versailles](Palace_of_Versailles "wikilink") establishing the
fashionable [Louis XIV style](Louis_XIV_style "wikilink") at a time when
France was becoming a cultural reference point for all of Europe.[^344]

French artists developed the [rococo](rococo "wikilink") style in the
early 18th century, as a more intimate evolution of the Baroque style,
the works of the court-endorsed artists [Antoine
Watteau](Antoine_Watteau "wikilink"), [François
Boucher](François_Boucher "wikilink") and [Jean-Honoré
Fragonard](Jean-Honoré_Fragonard "wikilink") being the most
representative in the country. The French Revolution brought great
changes, as Napoleon favoured artists of [neoclassic
style](Neoclassicism "wikilink") such as [Jacques-Louis
David](Jacques-Louis_David "wikilink") and the highly influential
[Académie des Beaux-Arts](Académie_des_Beaux-Arts "wikilink") defined
the style known as [Academism](Academic_art "wikilink").

In the second part of the 19th century, France\'s influence over
painting grew, with the development of new styles of painting such as
[Impressionism](Impressionism "wikilink") and
[Symbolism](Symbolist_painting "wikilink"). The most famous
impressionist painters of the period were [Camille
Pissarro](Camille_Pissarro "wikilink"), [Édouard
Manet](Édouard_Manet "wikilink"), [Edgar Degas](Edgar_Degas "wikilink"),
[Claude Monet](Claude_Monet "wikilink") and [Auguste
Renoir](Auguste_Renoir "wikilink").[^345] The second generation of
impressionist-style painters, [Paul Cézanne](Paul_Cézanne "wikilink"),
[Paul Gauguin](Paul_Gauguin "wikilink"),
[Toulouse-Lautrec](Toulouse-Lautrec "wikilink") and [Georges
Seurat](Georges_Seurat "wikilink"), were also at the avant-garde of
artistic evolutions,[^346] as well as the [fauvist](Fauvism "wikilink")
artists [Henri Matisse](Henri_Matisse "wikilink"), [André
Derain](André_Derain "wikilink") and [Maurice de
Vlaminck](Maurice_de_Vlaminck "wikilink").[^347][^348]

At the beginning of the 20th century, [Cubism](Cubism "wikilink") was
developed by [Georges Braque](Georges_Braque "wikilink") and the Spanish
painter [Pablo Picasso](Pablo_Picasso "wikilink"), who was living in
Paris.[^349] Other foreign artists also settled and worked in or near
Paris, such as [Vincent van Gogh](Vincent_van_Gogh "wikilink") and
[Wassily Kandinsky](Wassily_Kandinsky "wikilink").[^350][^351] In the
early 20th century and in particular during the interwar period, a loose
collection of foreign artists of whom many were Jewish formed the
[School of Paris](School_of_Paris "wikilink"). Amongst these artists
were [Marc Chagall](Marc_Chagall "wikilink"), [Amedeo
Modigliani](Amedeo_Modigliani "wikilink"), [Yitzhak
Frenel](Alexandre_Frenel "wikilink"), [Jules
Pascin](Jules_Pascin "wikilink") and others.[^352] The Second World War
led to the deaths or exile of multiple artists, resulting in the
relative fall of Paris\' domination and centrality in the world art
scene in favour of New York City.[^353]

### Architecture

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|French architecture}}
```

[thumb\|right\|upright=.7\|alt=Sainte Chapelle interior showing painted
stonework vaulting and stained glass\|[Saint
Louis\'](Louis_IX_of_France "wikilink")
[Sainte-Chapelle](Sainte-Chapelle "wikilink") represents the French
impact on religious
architecture.](File:Sainte_Chapelle_-_Upper_level_1.jpg "wikilink")
During the Middle Ages, many fortified castles were built by feudal
nobles to mark their powers. Some French castles that survive are
[Chinon](<Chinon_(castle)> "wikilink"), [Château
d\'Angers](Château_d'Angers "wikilink"), [Château de
Vincennes](Château_de_Vincennes "wikilink") and the [Cathar
castles](Cathar_castles "wikilink"). During this era, France had been
using [Romanesque architecture](Romanesque_architecture "wikilink") like
most of Western Europe.

[Gothic architecture](Gothic_architecture "wikilink"), originally named
_Opus Francigenum_ meaning \"French work\",[^354] was born in
[Île-de-France](Île-de-France "wikilink") and was the first French style
of architecture to be imitated throughout Europe.[^355] Northern France
is the home of some of the most important Gothic cathedrals and
basilicas, the first of these being the [Saint Denis
Basilica](Basilica_of_Saint-Denis "wikilink") (used as the royal
necropolis); other important French Gothic cathedrals are [Notre-Dame de
Chartres](Chartres_Cathedral "wikilink") and [Notre-Dame
d\'Amiens](Amiens_Cathedral "wikilink"). The kings were crowned in
another important Gothic church: [Notre-Dame de
Reims](Reims_Cathedral "wikilink").[^356]

The final victory in the Hundred Years\' War marked an important stage
in the evolution of French architecture. It was the time of the [French
Renaissance](French_Renaissance "wikilink") and several artists from
Italy were invited to the French court; many residential palaces were
built in the [Loire Valley](Loire_Valley "wikilink"), from 1450 as a
first reference the [Château de
Montsoreau](Château_de_Montsoreau "wikilink").[^357] Examples of such
residential castles include the [Château de
Chambord](Château_de_Chambord "wikilink"), the [Château de
Chenonceau](Château_de_Chenonceau "wikilink"), or the [Château
d\'Amboise](Château_d'Amboise "wikilink").

Following the Renaissance and the end of the Middle Ages, [Baroque
architecture](French_Baroque_architecture "wikilink") replaced the
traditional Gothic style. Baroque architecture found greater success in
the secular domain than in the religious one.[^358] In the secular
domain, the [Palace of Versailles](Palace_of_Versailles "wikilink") has
many Baroque features. [Jules
Hardouin-Mansart](Jules_Hardouin-Mansart "wikilink"), who designed the
extensions to Versailles, was one of the most influential French
architects of the Baroque era; he is famous for his dome at [Les
Invalides](Les_Invalides "wikilink").[^359] Some of the most
impressive`{{according to whom?|date=May 2026}}`{=mediawiki} provincial
Baroque architecture is found in places that were not yet French such as
[Place Stanislas](Place_Stanislas "wikilink") in
[Nancy](Nancy,_France "wikilink"). On the military architectural side,
[Vauban](Sébastien_Le_Prestre,_Marquis_of_Vauban "wikilink") designed
some of the most efficient fortresses in Europe and became an
influential military architect; as a result, imitations of his works can
be found all over Europe, the Americas, Russia and Turkey.[^360][^361]

After the Revolution, the [Republicans](Republicanism "wikilink")
favoured [Neoclassicism](Neoclassicism "wikilink") although it was
introduced in France before the revolution with such buildings as the
[Parisian Pantheon](Panthéon "wikilink") or the [Capitole de
Toulouse](Capitole_de_Toulouse "wikilink"). Built during the French
First Empire, the [Arc de Triomphe](Arc_de_Triomphe "wikilink") and
[Sainte Marie-Madeleine](Église_de_la_Madeleine "wikilink") represent
the best example of [Empire style](Empire_style "wikilink")
architecture.[^362] Under [Napoleon III](Napoleon_III "wikilink"), a
wave of urbanism and architecture was given birth; extravagant buildings
such as the neo-Baroque [Palais Garnier](Palais_Garnier "wikilink") were
built. The urban planning of the time was very organised and rigorous;
most notably, [Haussmann\'s renovation of
Paris](Haussmann's_renovation_of_Paris "wikilink"). The architecture
associated with this era is named [Second
Empire](<Second_Empire_(architecture)> "wikilink") in English, the term
being taken from the [Second French
Empire](Second_French_Empire "wikilink"). At this time there was a
strong Gothic resurgence across Europe and in France; the associated
architect was [Eugène Viollet-le-Duc](Eugène_Viollet-le-Duc "wikilink").
In the late 19th century, [Gustave Eiffel](Gustave_Eiffel "wikilink")
designed many bridges, such as the [Garabit
viaduct](Garabit_viaduct "wikilink"), and remains one of the most
influential bridge designers of his time, although he is best remembered
for the [Eiffel Tower](Eiffel_Tower "wikilink").

In the 20th century, French-Swiss architect [Le
Corbusier](Le_Corbusier "wikilink") designed several buildings in
France. More recently, French architects have combined both modern and
old architectural styles. The [Louvre
Pyramid](Louvre_Pyramid "wikilink") is an example of modern architecture
added to an older building. The most difficult buildings to integrate
within French cities are skyscrapers, as they are visible from afar. For
instance, in Paris since 1977, new buildings had to be under
`{{Convert| 37| m}}`{=mediawiki}.[^363] A significant number of
skyscrapers are located in the [La Défense](La_Défense "wikilink")
financial district.[^364] Other massive buildings that are a challenge
to integrate into their environment are large bridges; an example of the
way this has been done is the [Millau
Viaduct](Millau_Viaduct "wikilink"). Some famous modern French
architects include [Jean Nouvel](Jean_Nouvel "wikilink"), [Dominique
Perrault](Dominique_Perrault "wikilink"), [Christian de
Portzamparc](Christian_de_Portzamparc "wikilink") and [Paul
Andreu](Paul_Andreu "wikilink").

### Literature and philosophy {#literature_and_philosophy}

[thumb\|upright=.8\|[Victor Hugo](Victor_Hugo "wikilink"), a French
[Romantic](Romanticism "wikilink") writer and
politician](File:Victor_Hugo_by_Étienne_Carjat_1876_-_full.jpg "wikilink")

The earliest [French literature](French_literature "wikilink") dates
from the Middle Ages, when there were several languages and dialects,
and writers used their own spelling and grammar. Some authors of
medieval texts, such as _[Tristan and
Iseult](Tristan_and_Iseult "wikilink")_ and
_[Lancelot-Grail](Lancelot-Grail "wikilink"),_ are unknown. Three famous
medieval authors are [Chrétien de
Troyes](Chrétien_de_Troyes "wikilink"), [Christine de
Pizan](Christine_de_Pizan "wikilink") ([langue
d\'oïl](Langues_d'oïl "wikilink")), and Duke [William IX of
Aquitaine](William_IX_of_Aquitaine "wikilink") ([langue
d\'oc](Occitan_language "wikilink")). Much medieval [French
poetry](French_poetry "wikilink") and literature was inspired by the
legends of the [Carolingian cycle](Matter_of_France "wikilink"), such as
the _[Song of Roland](Song_of_Roland "wikilink")_ and the [chansons de
geste](chansons_de_geste "wikilink"). The _Roman de Renart_, written in
1175 by Perrout de Saint Cloude, tells the story of the medieval
character [Reynard](Reynard_the_Fox "wikilink") (\'the Fox\') and is
another example of early French writing. An important 16th-century
writer was [François Rabelais](François_Rabelais "wikilink"), who wrote
five popular early [picaresque](picaresque "wikilink") novels. Rabelais
was also in regular communication with [Marguerite de
Navarre](Marguerite_de_Navarre "wikilink"), author of the
_[Heptaméron](Heptaméron "wikilink")_.[^365] [Michel de
Montaigne](Michel_de_Montaigne "wikilink")\'s most famous work,
_[Essais](<Essays_(Montaigne)> "wikilink")\_, started a literary
genre.[^366]

French literature and poetry flourished during the 18th and 19th
centuries. [Denis Diderot](Denis_Diderot "wikilink") is best known as
the main editor of the _[Encyclopédie](Encyclopédie "wikilink")_, whose
aim was to sum up all the knowledge of his century and to fight
ignorance and [obscurantism](obscurantism "wikilink"). [Charles
Perrault](Charles_Perrault "wikilink") was a prolific writer of
children\'s fairy tales including _[Puss in
Boots](Puss_in_Boots "wikilink")_,
_[Cinderella](Cinderella "wikilink")_, _[Sleeping
Beauty](Sleeping_Beauty "wikilink")_ and
_[Bluebeard](Bluebeard "wikilink")_. At the start of the 19th century,
[symbolist poetry](<symbolism_(movement)> "wikilink") was an important
movement, with poets such as [Charles
Baudelaire](Charles_Baudelaire "wikilink"), [Paul
Verlaine](Paul_Verlaine "wikilink") and [Stéphane
Mallarmé](Stéphane_Mallarmé "wikilink").[^367]

[Victor Hugo](Victor_Hugo "wikilink") is sometimes seen as \"the
greatest French writer of all time\"[^368] for excelling in all literary
genres. Hugo\'s verse has been compared to that of
[Shakespeare](William_Shakespeare "wikilink"),
[Dante](Dante_Alighieri "wikilink") and [Homer](Homer "wikilink").[^369]
His novel _[Les Misérables](Les_Misérables "wikilink")_ is widely seen
as one of the greatest novels ever written,[^370] and _[The Hunchback of
Notre Dame](The_Hunchback_of_Notre-Dame "wikilink")_ has remained
immensely popular. Other major authors of the 19th century include
[Alexandre Dumas](Alexandre_Dumas "wikilink") (_[The Three
Musketeers](The_Three_Musketeers "wikilink")_ and _[The Count of Monte
Cristo](The_Count_of_Monte_Cristo "wikilink")_), [Jules
Verne](Jules_Verne "wikilink") (_[Twenty Thousand Leagues Under the
Seas](Twenty_Thousand_Leagues_Under_the_Seas "wikilink")_), [Émile
Zola](Émile_Zola "wikilink") (_[Les
Rougon-Macquart](Les_Rougon-Macquart "wikilink")_), [Honoré de
Balzac](Honoré_de_Balzac "wikilink") (_[La Comédie
humaine](La_Comédie_humaine "wikilink")_), [Guy de
Maupassant](Guy_de_Maupassant "wikilink"), [Théophile
Gautier](Théophile_Gautier "wikilink") and
[Stendhal](Stendhal "wikilink") (_[The Red and the
Black](The_Red_and_the_Black "wikilink")_, _[The Charterhouse of
Parma](The_Charterhouse_of_Parma "wikilink")_).

In the early 20th century France was a haven for literary freedom.[^371]
Works banned for obscenity in the US, the UK and other Anglophone
nations were published in France decades before they were available in
the respective authors\' home countries.[^372] The French were
disinclined to punish literary figures for their writing, and
prosecutions were rare.[^373] Important writers of the 20th century
include [Marcel Proust](Marcel_Proust "wikilink"), [Louis-Ferdinand
Céline](Louis-Ferdinand_Céline "wikilink"), [Jean
Cocteau](Jean_Cocteau "wikilink"), [Albert
Camus](Albert_Camus "wikilink"), and [Jean-Paul
Sartre](Jean-Paul_Sartre "wikilink"). [Antoine de
Saint-Exupéry](Antoine_de_Saint-Exupéry "wikilink") wrote _[The Little
Prince](The_Little_Prince "wikilink")_, which is one of the best selling
books in history.[^374][^375]

#### Philosophy

Medieval [French philosophy](French_philosophy "wikilink") was dominated
by [Scholasticism](Scholasticism "wikilink") until the emergence of
[Humanism](Humanism_in_France "wikilink") in the Renaissance. [Modern
philosophy](Modern_philosophy "wikilink") began in the 17th century with
[René Descartes](René_Descartes "wikilink"), [Blaise
Pascal](Blaise_Pascal "wikilink") and [Nicolas
Malebranche](Nicolas_Malebranche "wikilink"). Descartes was the first
[Western philosopher](Western_philosophy "wikilink") since ancient times
to attempt to build a philosophical system from the ground up rather
than building on the work of predecessors.[^376][^377] Major
philosophical contributions in the 18th century included
[Voltaire](Voltaire "wikilink"), who came to embody the Enlightenment,
and [Jean-Jacques Rousseau](Jean-Jacques_Rousseau "wikilink"), whose
work highly influenced the French Revolution.[^378][^379] French
philosophers made major contributions to the field in the 20th century
including the [existentialist](Existentialism "wikilink") works of
[Simone de Beauvoir](Simone_de_Beauvoir "wikilink"), Camus, and
Sartre.[^380] Other influential contributors include the moral and
political works of [Simone Weil](Simone_Weil "wikilink"), contributions
to [structuralism](structuralism "wikilink") including from [Claude
Lévi-Strauss](Claude_Lévi-Strauss "wikilink") and the
[post-structuralist](Post-structuralism "wikilink") works by [Michel
Foucault](Michel_Foucault "wikilink").[^381][^382]

### Music

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Music of France}}
```

[thumb\|upright=.8\|[Claude Debussy](Claude_Debussy "wikilink"), a
French composer](File:Claude_Debussy_by_Atelier_Nadar.jpg "wikilink")
[French music](Music_of_France "wikilink") experienced a golden age in
the 17th century thanks to [Louis XIV](Louis_XIV "wikilink"), who
employed talented musicians and composers in the royal court. Composers
of this period include [Marc-Antoine
Charpentier](Marc-Antoine_Charpentier "wikilink"), [François
Couperin](François_Couperin "wikilink"), [Michel-Richard
Delalande](Michel-Richard_Delalande "wikilink"), [Jean-Baptiste
Lully](Jean-Baptiste_Lully "wikilink") and [Marin
Marais](Marin_Marais "wikilink"), all of them composers at the court.
After the death of Louis XIV, French musical creation lost dynamism, but
in the next century the music of [Jean-Philippe
Rameau](Jean-Philippe_Rameau "wikilink") achieved some prestige. Rameau
became the dominant composer of [French opera](French_opera "wikilink")
and the leading French composer of the harpsichord.[^383]

In the field of [classical music](classical_music "wikilink"), France
has produced notable composers such as [Gabriel
Fauré](Gabriel_Fauré "wikilink"), [Claude
Debussy](Claude_Debussy "wikilink"), [Maurice
Ravel](Maurice_Ravel "wikilink"), and [Hector
Berlioz](Hector_Berlioz "wikilink"). Debussy and Ravel are the most
prominent figures associated with [Impressionist
music](Impressionism_in_music "wikilink"). The two composers invented
new musical forms[^384][^385][^386][^387] and new sounds. Debussy was
among the most influential composers of the late 19th and early 20th
centuries, and his use of non-traditional scales and
[chromaticism](chromaticism "wikilink") influenced many composers who
followed.[^388] His music is noted for its sensory content and frequent
usage of [atonality](atonality "wikilink"). [Erik
Satie](Erik_Satie "wikilink") was a key member of the early-20th-century
Parisian [avant-garde](avant-garde "wikilink"). [Francis
Poulenc](Francis_Poulenc "wikilink")\'s best-known works are his piano
suite _[Trois mouvements perpétuels](Mouvements_perpétuels "wikilink")_
(1919), the ballet _[Les Biches](Les_Biches "wikilink")_ (1923), the
_[Concert champêtre](Concert_champêtre "wikilink")_ (1928) for
harpsichord and orchestra, the opera _[Dialogues des
Carmélites](Dialogues_of_the_Carmelites "wikilink")_ (1957) and the
_[Gloria](<Gloria_(Poulenc)> "wikilink")\_ (1959) for soprano, choir and
orchestra. In the middle of the 20th century, [Maurice
Ohana](Maurice_Ohana "wikilink"), [Pierre
Schaeffer](Pierre_Schaeffer "wikilink") and [Pierre
Boulez](Pierre_Boulez "wikilink") contributed to the evolution of
[contemporary classical
music](contemporary_classical_music "wikilink").[^389]

French music then followed the rapid emergence of pop and rock music in
the middle of the 20th century. Although English-speaking creations
achieved popularity in the country, [French pop
music](French_popular_music "wikilink"), known as _[chanson
française](chanson_française "wikilink")_, has also remained very
popular. Among the most important French artists of the century are
[Édith Piaf](Édith_Piaf "wikilink"), [Georges
Brassens](Georges_Brassens "wikilink"), [Léo
Ferré](Léo_Ferré "wikilink"), [Charles
Aznavour](Charles_Aznavour "wikilink") and [Serge
Gainsbourg](Serge_Gainsbourg "wikilink").[^390] Modern pop music has
seen the rise of popular [French hip hop](French_hip_hop "wikilink"),
[French rock](French_rock "wikilink"),
[techno](techno "wikilink")/[funk](funk "wikilink"), and
[turntablists](turntablist "wikilink")/DJs. Although there are very few
rock bands in France compared to English-speaking countries,[^391] bands
such as [Noir Désir](Noir_Désir "wikilink"), [Mano
Negra](<Mano_Negra_(band)> "wikilink"),
[Niagara](<Niagara_(band)> "wikilink"), [Les Rita
Mitsouko](Les_Rita_Mitsouko "wikilink") and more recently
[Superbus](<Superbus_(band)> "wikilink"),
[Phoenix](<Phoenix_(French_band)> "wikilink") and
[Gojira](<Gojira_(band)> "wikilink"),[^392] or [Shaka
Ponk](Shaka_Ponk "wikilink"), have reached worldwide popularity.

### Cinema

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Cinema of France}}
```

![A _[Palme d\'Or](Palme_d'Or "wikilink")_ from the [Cannes Film
Festival](Cannes_Film_Festival "wikilink"), one of the \"[Big
Three](Film_festival "wikilink")\" film festivals alongside the [Venice
Film Festival](Venice_Film_Festival "wikilink") and [Berlin
International Film
Festival](Berlin_International_Film_Festival "wikilink")[^393][^394][^395]](Palmed'or.jpg "A Palme d'Or from the Cannes
Film Festival, one of the "Big Three" film festivals alongside the Venice Film Festival and Berlin International Film
Festival")

France has historical and strong links with
[cinema](Filmmaking "wikilink"), with two Frenchmen, Auguste and Louis
Lumière (known as the [Lumière
Brothers](Auguste_and_Louis_Lumière "wikilink")) credited with creating
cinema in 1895.[^399] The world\'s first female filmmaker, [Alice
Guy-Blaché](Alice_Guy-Blaché "wikilink"), was also from France.[^400]
Several important cinematic movements, including the late 1950s and
1960s [Nouvelle Vague](Nouvelle_Vague "wikilink"), began in the country.
It is noted for having a strong film industry, due in part to
protections afforded by the government. France remains a leader in
filmmaking, `{{As of|2015|lc=y}}`{=mediawiki} producing more films than
any other European country.[^401][^402] The nation also hosts the
[Cannes Festival](Cannes_Festival "wikilink"), one of the most important
and famous film festivals in the world.[^403][^404]

Apart from its strong and innovative film tradition, France has also
been a gathering spot for artists from across Europe and the world. For
this reason, French cinema is sometimes intertwined with the cinema of
foreign nations. Directors from nations such as Poland ([Roman
Polanski](Roman_Polanski "wikilink"), [Krzysztof
Kieślowski](Krzysztof_Kieślowski "wikilink"), [Andrzej
Żuławski](Andrzej_Żuławski "wikilink")), Argentina ([Gaspar
Noé](Gaspar_Noé "wikilink"), [Edgardo
Cozarinsky](Edgardo_Cozarinsky "wikilink")), Russia ([Alexandre
Alexeieff](Alexandre_Alexeieff "wikilink"), [Anatole
Litvak](Anatole_Litvak "wikilink")), Austria ([Michael
Haneke](Michael_Haneke "wikilink")) and Georgia ([Géla
Babluani](Géla_Babluani "wikilink"), [Otar
Iosseliani](Otar_Iosseliani "wikilink")) are prominent in the ranks of
French cinema. Conversely, French directors have had prolific and
influential careers in other countries, such as [Luc
Besson](Luc_Besson "wikilink"), [Jacques
Tourneur](Jacques_Tourneur "wikilink") or [Francis
Veber](Francis_Veber "wikilink") in the [United
States](Cinema_of_the_United_States "wikilink"). Although the French
film market is dominated by Hollywood, France is the only nation in the
world where American films make up the smallest share of total film
revenues, at 50%, compared with 77% in Germany and 69% in Japan.[^405]
French films account for 35% of the total film revenues of France, which
is the highest percentage of national film revenues in the developed
world outside the United States, compared to 14% in Spain and 8% in the
UK.[^406] In 2013, France was the second-largest exporter of films in
the world, after the United States.[^407]

As part of its advocacy of [cultural
exception](cultural_exception "wikilink"), a political concept of
treating culture differently from other commercial products,[^408]
France succeeded in convincing all EU members to refuse to include
culture and audiovisuals in the list of liberalised sectors of the WTO
in 1993.[^409] This decision was confirmed in a vote by
[UNESCO](UNESCO "wikilink") in 2005.[^410]

### Fashion

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|French fashion}}
```

![[Chanel](Chanel "wikilink")'s headquarters on [Place
Vendôme](Place_Vendôme "wikilink"),
Paris](Channel_headquarters_bordercropped.jpg "Chanel's headquarters on Place Vendôme, Paris")

Fashion has been an important industry and cultural export of France
since the 17th century, and modern \"[haute
couture](haute_couture "wikilink")\" originated in Paris in the 1860s.
Today, Paris, along with London, Milan, and New York City, is considered
one of the world\'s [fashion capitals](fashion_capital "wikilink"), and
the city is home or headquarters to many of the premier fashion houses.
The expression Haute couture is, in France, a legally protected name,
guaranteeing certain quality standards.

The association of France with fashion and style
(`{{Langx|fr|link=no|la mode}}`{=mediawiki}) dates largely to the reign
of [Louis XIV](Louis_XIV "wikilink").[^411] France renewed its dominance
of the high fashion
(`{{Langx|fr|link=no|couture {{Noitalic|or}} haute couture}}`{=mediawiki})
industry in the years 1860--1960 through the establishment of the great
[couturier](couturier "wikilink") houses such as
[Chanel](Chanel "wikilink"), [Dior](Christian_Dior_S.A. "wikilink"), and
[Givenchy](Givenchy "wikilink"). The French perfume industry is the
world leader in its sector and is centred on the town of
[Grasse](Grasse "wikilink").[^412]

According to 2017 data compiled by [Deloitte](Deloitte "wikilink"),
[Louis Vuitton Moet Hennessey](Louis_Vuitton_Moet_Hennessey "wikilink")
(LVMH), a French brand, is the largest luxury company in the world by
sales, selling more than twice the amount of its nearest
competitor.[^413] Moreover, France also possesses 3 of the top 10 luxury
goods companies by sales ([LVMH](LVMH "wikilink"), [Kering
SA](Kering_SA "wikilink"), [L\'Oréal](L'Oréal "wikilink")), more than
any other country in the world.[^414]

### Media

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Telecommunications in France|Censorship in France}}
```

[thumb\|upright=.7\|_[Le Figaro](Le_Figaro "wikilink")_ was founded in
1826 and is still considered a [newspaper of
record](newspaper_of_record "wikilink").[^415]](File:Siège_Figaro,_14_boulevard_Haussmann,_Paris_9e.jpg "wikilink")

In 2021, regional daily newspapers, such as
_[Ouest-France](Ouest-France "wikilink")_, _[Sud
Ouest](<Sud_Ouest_(newspaper)> "wikilink")_, _[La Voix du
Nord](<La_Voix_du_Nord_(daily)> "wikilink")_, _[Dauphiné
Libéré](Dauphiné_Libéré "wikilink")_, _[Le
Télégramme](Le_Télégramme "wikilink")_, and _[Le
Progrès](Le_Progrès "wikilink")_, more than doubled the sales of
national newspapers, such as _[Le Monde](Le_Monde "wikilink")_, _[Le
Figaro](Le_Figaro "wikilink")_, _[L\'Équipe](L'Équipe "wikilink")_
(sports), _[Le Parisien](Le_Parisien "wikilink")_, and _[Les
Echos](<Les_Echos_(France)> "wikilink")\_ (finance). Free dailies,
distributed in metropolitan centres, continue to increase their market
share.[^416] The sector of weekly magazines includes more than 400
specialised weekly magazines published in the country.[^417]

The most influential news magazines are the left-wing _[Le Nouvel
Observateur](L'Obs "wikilink")_, centrist
_[L\'Express](<L'Express_(France)> "wikilink")_ and right-wing _[Le
Point](Le_Point "wikilink")_ (in 2009 more than 400,000 copies),[^418]
but the highest circulation numbers for weeklies are attained by TV
magazines and by women\'s magazines, among them _[Marie
Claire](Marie_Claire "wikilink")_ and
_[Elle](<Elle_(magazine)> "wikilink")_, which have foreign versions.
Influential weeklies also include investigative and satirical papers
_[Le Canard Enchaîné](Le_Canard_Enchaîné "wikilink")_ and _[Charlie
Hebdo](Charlie_Hebdo "wikilink")_, as well as _[Paris
Match](Paris_Match "wikilink")\_. As in most industrialised nations, the
print media have been affected by a [severe
crisis](Newspaper_crisis "wikilink") with the rise of the internet. In
2008, the government launched a major initiative to help the sector
reform and become financially independent,[^419][^420] but in 2009 it
had to give €600,000 to help the print media cope with the [2008
financial crisis](2008_financial_crisis "wikilink"), in addition to
existing subsidies.[^421] In 1974, after years of centralised monopoly
on radio and television, the governmental agency [ORTF](ORTF "wikilink")
was split into several national institutions, but the three
already-existing TV channels and four national radio
stations[^422][^423] remained under state control. It was only in 1981
that the government allowed free broadcasting in the territory.[^424]

Some consider [hate speech laws in
France](hate_speech_laws_in_France "wikilink") to be too broad or
severe, undermining [freedom of
speech](freedom_of_speech "wikilink").[^425]

### Cuisine

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|French cuisine}}
```

![[French wines](French_wine "wikilink") are usually made to accompany
French
cuisine.](French_taste_of_wines.JPG "French wines are usually made to accompany French cuisine.")

Different regions have different styles. In the north, butter and cream
are common ingredients, whereas [olive oil](olive_oil "wikilink") is
more commonly used in the south.[^426] Each region of France has
traditional specialties: [cassoulet](cassoulet "wikilink") in the
southwest, [choucroute](Choucroute_garnie "wikilink") in Alsace,
[quiche](quiche "wikilink") in the [Lorraine
region](Lorraine "wikilink"), [beef
bourguignon](beef_bourguignon "wikilink") in
[Burgundy](Burgundy "wikilink"), [Provençal](Provence "wikilink")
[tapenade](tapenade "wikilink"), etc. France is most famous for its
[wines](French_wine "wikilink")[^427] and
[cheeses](List_of_French_cheeses "wikilink"), which are often named for
the territory where they are produced
([AOC](Appellation_d'origine_contrôlée "wikilink")).[^428][^429] A meal
typically consists of three courses, _entrée_ (\'starter\'), _plat
principal_ (\'main course\'), and _fromage_ (\'cheese\') or _dessert_,
sometimes with a salad served before the cheese or dessert.

French cuisine is also regarded as a key element of the [quality of
life](quality_of_life "wikilink") and the attractiveness of France. A
French publication, the _[Michelin Guide](Michelin_Guide "wikilink")_,
awards _Michelin stars_ for excellence to a select few
establishments.[^430][^431] The acquisition or loss of a star can have
dramatic effects on the success of a restaurant. By 2006, the _Michelin
Guide_ had awarded 620 stars to French restaurants.[^432]

In addition to its wine tradition, France is also a major producer of
beer and rum. The three main French brewing regions are Alsace (60% of
national production), Nord-Pas-de-Calais, and Lorraine. [French
rum](List_of_French_rums "wikilink") is made in distilleries on islands
in the Atlantic and Indian oceans.[^433][^434]

### Sports

```{=mediawiki}
{{Main|Sport in France}}
```

![Starting in 1903, the [Tour de France](Tour_de_France "wikilink") is
the most prestigious of [Grands Tours](Grand_Tour_(cycling) "wikilink"),
and the world's most famous cycling
race.[^435]](Stage_20_Tour_de_France_2024_Col_de_la_Couillole_42.jpg "Starting in 1903, the Tour de France is the most prestigious of Grands Tours, and the world's most famous cycling race.")

France hosts \"the world\'s biggest annual sporting event\", the annual
cycling race [Tour de France](Tour_de_France "wikilink").[^437] Other
popular sports played in France include
[football](Association_football "wikilink"), [judo](judo "wikilink"),
[tennis](tennis "wikilink"),[^438] [rugby
union](rugby_union "wikilink")[^439] and
[pétanque](pétanque "wikilink"). France has hosted events such as the
[1938](1938_FIFA_World_Cup "wikilink") and [1998 FIFA World
Cups](1998_FIFA_World_Cup "wikilink"),[^440] the [2007 Rugby World
Cup](2007_Rugby_World_Cup "wikilink"),[^441] and the [2023 Rugby World
Cup](2023_Rugby_World_Cup "wikilink").[^442] The country also hosted the
[1960 European Nations\' Cup](1960_European_Nations'_Cup "wikilink"),
[UEFA Euro 1984](UEFA_Euro_1984 "wikilink"), [UEFA Euro
2016](UEFA_Euro_2016 "wikilink") and [2019 FIFA Women\'s World
Cup](2019_FIFA_Women's_World_Cup "wikilink"). The [Stade de
France](Stade_de_France "wikilink") in
[Saint-Denis](Saint-Denis,_Seine-Saint-Denis "wikilink") is France\'s
largest stadium and was the venue for the 1998 FIFA World Cup and 2007
Rugby World Cup finals. Since 1923, France is famous for its [24 Hours
of Le Mans](24_Hours_of_Le_Mans "wikilink") sports car [endurance
race](<endurance_racing_(motorsport)> "wikilink").[^443] Several major
tennis tournaments take place in France, including the [Paris
Masters](Paris_Masters "wikilink") and the [French
Open](French_Open "wikilink"), one of the four [Grand
Slam](<Grand_Slam_(tennis)> "wikilink") tournaments. French [martial
arts](martial_arts "wikilink") include [Savate](Savate "wikilink") and
[Fencing](Fencing "wikilink").

France has a close association with the Modern [Olympic
Games](Olympic_Games "wikilink"); it was a French aristocrat, Baron
[Pierre de Coubertin](Pierre_de_Coubertin "wikilink"), who suggested the
Games\' revival, at the end of the 19th century.[^444][^445] Paris
hosted the second Games [in
1900](1900_Summer_Olympics "wikilink"),[^446] and has hosted the
Olympics on five further occasions: the [1924 Summer
Olympics](1924_Summer_Olympics "wikilink"), the [2024 Summer
Olympics](2024_Summer_Olympics "wikilink") both in Paris[^447] and three
[Winter Games](Winter_Olympic_Games "wikilink")
([1924](1924_Winter_Olympics "wikilink") in
[Chamonix](Chamonix "wikilink"), [1968](1968_Winter_Olympics "wikilink")
in [Grenoble](Grenoble "wikilink") and
[1992](1992_Winter_Olympics "wikilink") in
[Albertville](Albertville "wikilink")).[^448] France introduced Olympics
for deaf people (Deaflympics) in
[1924](1924_Summer_Deaflympics "wikilink").[^449]

Both the [national football
team](France_national_football_team "wikilink") and the [national rugby
union team](France_national_rugby_union_team "wikilink") are nicknamed
\"_Les Bleus_\". Football is the most popular sport in France, with over
1,800,000 registered players and over 18,000 registered clubs.[^450]
[Rugby union](Rugby_union "wikilink") is popular, particularly in Paris
and the southwest of France.[^451] The national rugby union team has
competed at every [Rugby World Cup](Rugby_World_Cup "wikilink"); it
takes part in the annual [Six Nations
Championship](Six_Nations_Championship "wikilink").

The French Open, also called Roland-Garros, is a major
[tennis](tennis "wikilink") tournament held over two weeks between late
May and early June at the [Stade
Roland-Garros](Stade_Roland_Garros "wikilink") in Paris. It is the
premier clay court tennis championship event in the world and the second
of four annual [Grand Slam](<Grand_Slam_(tennis)> "wikilink")
tournaments.[^452]
